A generalized cluster structure on G ​ L n GL_{n} via birational Poisson maps
Misha Gekhtman, Michael Shapiro, Alek Vainshtein

Abstract
. In a recent work, we constructed a rational map from a simple Lie group 𝒢 {\mathcal{G}} to itself that intertwines the standard Poisson–Lie structure on 𝒢 {\mathcal{G}} with a Poisson homogeneous one defined by a pair of quasi-triangular solutions to the classical Yang–Baxter equation(CYBE) known as R-matrices. We also showed, in the case of S ​ L n SL_{n} , that if the combinatorial Belavin–Drinfeld data associated with these R-matrices satisfies certain aperiodicity conditions , the map is, in fact, birational and can be used to obtain an initial cluster for an exotic cluster structure on S ​ L n SL_{n} via the pullback of Berenstein–Fomin–Zelevinsky cluster variables. The same strategy was later used by the first author and D. Voloshyn to describe generalized cluster structures compatible with the Poisson dual of the Poisson–Lie bracket defined by a quasi- triangular R-matrix. In this paper we further promote the use of birational Poisson maps in constructing generalized cluster structures by applying it in the situation when the aperiodicity condition is not satisfied. To this end, we describe a generalized cluster structure on G ​ L n GL_{n} compatible with the Poisson homogeneous bracket defined by two Cremmer–Gervais solutions to the CYBE related via conjugation by the longest element of the Weyl group. The key ingredient to our construction is a birational map that connects the bracket under consideration with two other Poisson brackets: the Poisson dual to Cremmer–Gervais Poisson–Lie bracket on G ​ L n − 1 GL_{n-1} and the bracket on a certain space of complex rational functions of one variable closely related to cluster algebraic interpretation of Coxeter–Toda flows. New notions of a regular pullback of a seed and of an almost-cluster structure whose detailed description are given in a separate note also play an important role in our construction.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
53D17, 13F60

1.
Introduction

A search for cluster structures in rings of regular functions on algebraic varieties has been among the most active directions of research in the theory of cluster algebras starting from the time they were discovered by Fomin and Zelevinsky. Two approaches to constructing cluster structures can be clearly distinguished in the literature. One relies on the combinatorics of Weyl and braid groups and has been successfully applied to a number of varieties, particularly those appearing in Lie theory: from Grassmannians
[
23
]
and double Bruhat cells of a simply-connected simple Lie group
[
2
]
in the early days of cluster algebras to the recent works on cluster structures on braid
varieties
[
4
]
. The second approach was initiated by the authors in
[
9
]
where the notion Poisson structures compatible with cluster transformations was introduced and the strategy of constructing a cluster structure in the ring of regular functions on a Poisson variety starting from a
regular log-canonical
chart was first implemented in the case of Grassmannians. The Poisson bracket in this case was the Poisson homogeneous bracket with respect to the natural action of
GL_{n}
equipped with the
standard Poisson–Lie bracket
, and the resulting cluster structure and the one from
[
23
]
turned out to coincide. Similarly, in
[
10
, Ch. 4.3]
we have shown that the cluster structure of
[
2
]
on double Bruhat cells of a simply-connected simple Lie group
{\mathcal{G}}
is compatible with the standard Poisson–Lie structure on
{\mathcal{G}}
.

But what happens if one modifies the Poisson bracket on the underlying variety? Does a compatible cluster structure still exist, and if so, how is it related to the original one? We posed this question, in the case of simple Poisson–Lie groups, in
[
12
]
and, while observing that already in
SL_{2}
there exists a Poisson–Lie bracket not compatible with any cluster structure, formulated a conjecture on the existence of compatible cluster structures for Poisson–-Lie brackets corresponding to quasi-triangular solutions of the classical Yang–Baxter equation (CYBE) classified by Belavin and Drinfeld in
[
1
]
. These solutions to CYBE, called R-matrices, are parametrized by discrete data consisting of an isometry between two subsets of positive roots in the root system of the Lie algebra of
{\mathcal{G}}
and a continuous parameter that can be described as an element of the tensor square of the Cartan subalgebra that satisfies a system of linear equations governed by the discrete data. The discrete data determines a
Belavin–Drinfeld (BD) class
of R-matrices and corresponding Poisson–Lie brackets, and continuous data specifies
a particular R-matrix and a bracket within this class. Given two such brackets on
{\mathcal{G}}
associated with representatives of two BD classes, one can define a Poisson–Lie group

{\mathcal{G}}\times{\mathcal{G}}
equipped with the direct product Poisson structure and then construct a Poisson-homogeneous structure on
{\mathcal{G}}
with respect to the action of
{\mathcal{G}}\times{\mathcal{G}}
by right and left multiplication.

In the series of papers culminating in
[
18
]
,
[
19
]
, we worked on an extended version of the conjecture
of
[
12
]
:
for any Poisson-homogeneous bracket associated with an arbitrary pair of BD data,
there exists a compatible regular complete, possibly generalized, cluster structure in the ring of regular functions on
{\mathcal{G}}
.
In
[
18
]
,
[
19
]
, we proved the conjecture for
SL_{n}
in the case of
aperiodic
BD data. Here aperiodicity means that a certain map
\delta
naturally induced by the discrete part of the BD data on simple positive roots has no periodic orbits.
The geometric meaning of the aperiodicity condition was clarified in
[
19
]
where we adopted a new approach to constructing a regular log-canonical chart on
{\mathcal{G}}
for a Poisson-homogeneous bracket under consideration, which constitutes the first and crucial step in uncovering a cluster structure we are looking for. In contrast with
[
18
]
, where an
ad hoc
ansatz was used to identify an initial log-canonical family of functions and the proof consisted of a long direct computation, in
[
19
]
we constructed and utilized a rational Poisson map
h^{r,r^{\prime}}
between two copies of
{\mathcal{G}}
endowed with two different Poisson-homogeneous structures. One is
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}_{r,r^{\prime}}
determined by a pair of R-matrices from two arbitrary BD classes. The other,
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}_{r,r^{\prime}}^{\rm st}
, corresponds to two R-matrices from the
standard
BD class whose Cartan parts match those of
r,r^{\prime}
. The map
h^{r,r^{\prime}}
maps

({\mathcal{G}},{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}_{r,r^{\prime}}^{\rm st})
to
({\mathcal{G}},{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}_{r,r^{\prime}})
. In the
SL_{n}
case with aperiodic BD data,
h^{r,r^{\prime}}
is birational and we are able to pull back a conveniently chosen cluster in the standard cluster structure on
{\mathcal{G}}
to obtain an initial seed for a cluster structure compatible with
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}_{r,r^{\prime}}
.

In
[
17
]
, we considered an example of a non-aperiodic BD data in
SL_{6}
to illustrate the emergence of generalized cluster exchange relations in this case and to sketch a conjectural construction of a compatible generalized cluster structure. We will revisit this construction in future publication.
It is important, however, to emphasize a key point illustrated by the example considered in
[
17
]
. Namely, in the non-aperiodic case for
SL_{n}
, periodic orbits of the map
\delta
on the set of positive simple roots induced by the BD data are divided into equivalence classes. Each family gives rise to an infinite periodic staircase matrix whose generalized characteristic polynomial provides coefficients of the corresponding generalized cluster exchange relation.

In this paper we shed additional light on generalized cluster structures in
SL_{n}
arising in a non-aperiodic case by providing a comprehensive analysis of a
generalized cluster structure
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
on
SL_{n}
(and its extensions to
GL_{n}
and
\operatorname{Mat}_{n}
)
compatible with
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}={\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}_{r,r^{\prime}}
, where
r
and
r^{\prime}
are two Cremmer–Gervais solutions to the CYBE related via conjugation by the longest element of the Weyl group
.
We chose this example because it represents a “purely” non-aperiodic case in a sense that the corresponding map
\delta
has only periodic orbits which form a single equivalence class. In a way, this choice of a non-aperiodic pair
(r,r^{\prime})
mirrors the progress of our systematic study of the aperiodic case which started, in
[
13
,
14
]
, with the description of the exotic cluster structure compatible with the Cremmer–Gervais Poisson–Lie bracket.

This work further promotes an approach based on using birational Poisson maps to recover a new (generalized) cluster structure from known ones.
Following
[
19
]
, this approach was also applied in
[
21
]
to describe generalized cluster structures compatible with Poisson dual
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{\dagger}_{r}
of the Poisson–Lie bracket defined by a quasi-triangular solution
r
to the CYBE. We use the construction in
[
21
]
as a crucial ingredient in defining a birational map
\Psi
that connects the bracket
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
defined above to two other Poisson brackets: the Poisson dual
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{\dagger}
to Cremmer–Gervais Poisson–Lie bracket on
SL_{n-1}
,
and the bracket
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{T}
on a certain space
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
of complex rational functions of one variable closely related to the one we studied in
[
11
]
in the context of cluster algebraic interpretation of Bäcklund–Darboux transformations of the Coxeter–Toda flows. Using a notion of a
regular pullback of a seed
, we apply
\Psi
to obtain an initial
seed for
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}_{n}

from initial seeds of structures compatible with
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{\dagger}
and
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{T}
, where the former (generalized) was constructed
in
[
21
]
and the latter is a slight modification of the one in
[
11
]
. We expect the map

(SL_{n},{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}})\to(SL_{n-1},{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{\dagger})
featured in our construction of
\Psi
to have an analogue for every equivalence class of periodic orbits of
\delta
for any non-aperiodic BD data, with the target space
(SL_{k},{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{\dagger})
, where
k
is determined by the data but

{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{\dagger}
is the same as in the current paper. The question of what the Poisson space
(\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{n},{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{T})
should be replaced with so that an analogue of the map
\Psi
could be defined for any non-aperiodic BD data is one of the main directions of our future research.

The paper is organized as follows. Section
2
begins with a brief overview of the necessary background on Poisson–Lie groups and Poisson-homogeneous structures on simple Lie groups arising from the
Belavin–Drinfeld classification of quasi-triangular
r
-matrices, and on generalized cluster structures compatible with Poisson brackets. We also discuss, in Section
2.3
, the notion of a
regular pullback of a seed
which plays an important role in our main construction; a more detailed treatment of this subject is contained in a separate paper
[
20
]
. In Section
2.4
we define the Poisson homogeneous bracket

{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
at the center of our investigation—it corresponds to the non-aperiodic pair of the “opposite” Cremmer–Gervais
data—and formulate our main result, Theorem
2.2
on the existence of a generalized cluster structure
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
compatible with
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
. The rest of Section
2
outlines the proof of the main theorem that hinges on definition and properties of a birational map
\Psi
that connects the bracket
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
with two other Poisson brackets: the Poisson dual
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{\dagger}
to Cremmer–Gervais Poisson–Lie bracket on
GL_{n-1}
, and the bracket
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{T}
on a certain space
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
of complex rational functions of one variable closely related to the one we studied in
[
11
]
in the context of cluster algebraic interpretation of Bäcklund–Darboux transformations of Coxeter–Toda flows. Using a regular pullback via
\Psi
of initial clusters for the structures compatible with
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{\dagger}
and
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{T}
,
we obtain an initial seed for
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
. The proof of the required modification of the construction of
[
11
]
is contained in the Appendix.

The results that constitute steps in the proof of the main theorem are stated in Section
2.7
. Their detailed proofs are presented in Sections
3
–
7
. These include several additional technical claims that we feel may prove useful beyond the specific context of this paper, e.g. Proposition
2.15
that elucidates a Poisson-geometric nature for frozen variables in a cluster structure compatible with a Poisson bracket.

2.
Preliminaries and Main Result

2.1.
Poisson–Lie groups

A reductive complex Lie group
{\mathcal{G}}
equipped with a Poisson bracket
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
is called a
Poisson–Lie group

if the multiplication map
{\mathcal{G}}\times{\mathcal{G}}\ni(X,Y)\mapsto XY\in{\mathcal{G}}

is Poisson. Perhaps, the most important class of Poisson–Lie groups
is the one associated with quasitriangular Lie bialgebras defined in terms of
classical R-matrices

(see, e. g.,
[
5
, Ch. 1]
,
[
22
]
and
[
26
]
for a detailed exposition of these structures).

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be the Lie algebra corresponding to
{\mathcal{G}}
,
\langle{\cdot},{\cdot}\rangle
be an invariant nondegenerate form on
\mathfrak{g}
,
and let
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
be the corresponding Casimir element.
For an arbitrary element
r=\sum_{i}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
denote

[[r,r]]=\sum_{i,j}[a_{i},a_{j}]\otimes b_{i}\otimes b_{j}+\sum_{i,j}a_{i}\otimes[b_{i},a_{j}]\otimes b_{j}+\sum_{i,j}a_{i}\otimes a_{j}\otimes[b_{i},b_{j}]

and
r^{21}=\sum_{i}b_{i}\otimes a_{i}
.
A
classical R-matrix
is an element
r\in\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
that satisfies

the classical Yang-Baxter equation (CYBE)

[[r,r]]=0

together with the condition
r+r^{21}=\mathfrak{t}
.
The Poisson–Lie bracket on
{\mathcal{G}}
that corresponds to
r
can be written as

(2.1)

\displaystyle\{f_{1},f_{2}\}_{r}

\displaystyle=\langle R_{+}(\nabla^{L}f_{1}),\nabla^{L}f_{2}\rangle-\langle R_{+}(\nabla^{R}f_{1}),\nabla^{R}f_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle R_{-}(\nabla^{L}f_{1}),\nabla^{L}f_{2}\rangle-\langle R_{-}(\nabla^{R}f_{1}),\nabla^{R}f_{2}\rangle,

where
R_{+},R_{-}\in\operatorname{End}\mathfrak{g}
are given by
\langle R_{+}\eta,\zeta\rangle=\langle r,\eta\otimes\zeta\rangle
,

-\langle R_{-}\zeta,\eta\rangle=\langle r,\eta\otimes\zeta\rangle
for any
\eta,\zeta\in\mathfrak{g}
and

\nabla^{L}
,
\nabla^{R}
are the right and the left gradients of functions on
{\mathcal{G}}
with respect to
\langle{\cdot},{\cdot}\rangle

defined by

\left\langle\nabla^{R}f(X),\xi\right\rangle=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}f(e^{t\xi}X),\quad\left\langle\nabla^{L}f(X),\xi\right\rangle=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}f(Xe^{t\xi})

for any
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
,
X\in{\mathcal{G}}
.

The double of
\mathfrak{g}
is

D(\mathfrak{g})=\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}
equipped with an invariant nondegenerate bilinear form

\langle\langle(\xi,\eta),(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})\rangle\rangle=\langle\xi,\xi^{\prime}\rangle-\langle\eta,\eta^{\prime}\rangle
.
Define subalgebras
{\mathfrak{d}}_{\pm}
of
D(\mathfrak{g})
by

{\mathfrak{d}}_{+}=\{(\xi,\xi):\xi\in\mathfrak{g}\}
and
{\mathfrak{d}}_{-}=\{(R_{+}(\xi),R_{-}(\xi)):\xi\in\mathfrak{g}\}
.
Using right and left gradients with respect to
\langle\langle{\cdot},{\cdot}\rangle\rangle
,
one can define
a Poisson–Lie structure on
D({\mathcal{G}})={\mathcal{G}}\times{\mathcal{G}}
, the
Drinfeld double
of the group
{\mathcal{G}}
, see
[
15
]

for details.

The diagonal subgroup
\{(X,X)\ :\ X\in{\mathcal{G}}\}
is a Poisson–Lie subgroup of
D({\mathcal{G}})
(whose Lie algebra is
{\mathfrak{d}}_{+}
) naturally isomorphic to
({\mathcal{G}},{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}_{r})
.
The group
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}
whose Lie algebra is
{\mathfrak{d}}_{-}
is a Poisson-Lie subgroup of
D({\mathcal{G}})
called the
dual Poisson–Lie group of
{\mathcal{G}}
.
The map
D({\mathcal{G}})\to{\mathcal{G}}
given by
(X,Y)\mapsto U=X^{-1}Y
induces another Poisson bracket on
{\mathcal{G}}
, see
[
22
]
; we denote this bracket
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{\dagger}_{r}
.
The image of the restriction of this map to
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}
is denoted

{\mathcal{G}}^{\dagger}=({\mathcal{G}},{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{\dagger}_{r})
.
As proved in
[
15
, Lemma 5.5]

(see equation (5.6) there), this bracket is given by

(2.2)

\{f_{1},f_{2}\}^{\dagger}_{r}=\langle R_{+}([\nabla f_{1},U]),[\nabla f_{2},U]\rangle-\langle[\nabla f_{1},U],\nabla f_{2}{\cdot}U\rangle.

As is explained in detail in
[
18
,
19
]
,
it is natural to consider the following generalization of the
bracket (
2.1
).
Let
r,\bar{r}
be two classical R-matrices, and
R_{+},\bar{R}_{+}
be the corresponding operators, then we write

(2.3)

\{f_{1},f_{2}\}_{r,\bar{r}}=\langle\bar{R}_{+}(\nabla^{L}f_{1}),\nabla^{L}f_{2}\rangle-\langle R_{+}(\nabla^{R}f_{1}),\nabla^{R}f_{2}\rangle.

By
[
22
, Proposition 12.11]
, the above expression defines a Poisson bracket, which is not Poisson–Lie unless
r=\bar{r}
,
in which case
\{f_{1},f_{2}\}_{r,r}
evidently coincides with
\{f_{1},f_{2}\}_{r}
.
The bracket (
2.3
) defines a Poisson homogeneous structure on
{\mathcal{G}}
with respect to the left and right multiplication by Poisson–Lie groups
({\mathcal{G}},{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}_{\bar{r}})
and

({\mathcal{G}},{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}_{r})
, respectively. It will be one of the main objects of our study in this paper.

The classification of classical R-matrices for simple complex Lie groups was given by Belavin and Drinfeld in
[
1
]
.
Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a simple complex Lie group,
\Phi
be the root system associated with its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
,
\Phi^{+}
be the set of positive roots, and
\Pi\subset\Phi^{+}
be the set of positive simple roots.
A
Belavin–Drinfeld triple

\mathbf{\Gamma}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\gamma)
(in what follows, a
BD triple
)
consists of two subsets
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
of
\Pi
and an isometry
\gamma{:\ }\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma_{2}
nilpotent in the
following sense: for every
\alpha\in\Gamma_{1}
there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\gamma^{j}(\alpha)\in\Gamma_{1}

for
j\in[0,m-1]
, but
\gamma^{m}(\alpha)\notin\Gamma_{1}
. We call the BD triple

\mathbf{\Gamma}^{*}=\{\Gamma_{2},\Gamma_{1},\gamma^{*}\}
with
\gamma^{*}{:\ }\Gamma_{2}\to\Gamma_{1}
defined via

\gamma^{*}(\gamma(\alpha))=\alpha
for
\alpha\in\Gamma_{1}
the
adjoint
of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
.
Recall that the longest element
w_{0}
of the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}
acts on
\Phi
and takes positive simple roots to negative simple roots. We define the
w_{0}
-conjugate
of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
via
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{w_{0}}=\{-w_{0}\Gamma_{1},-w_{0}\Gamma_{2},\gamma^{w_{0}}\}
with
\gamma^{w_{0}}{:\ }-w_{0}\Gamma_{1}\to-w_{0}\Gamma_{2}

given by
\gamma^{w_{0}}(-w_{0}\alpha)=-w_{0}\gamma(\alpha)
for
\alpha\in\Gamma_{1}
.

The isometry
\gamma
yields an isomorphism, also denoted by
\gamma
, between the Lie subalgebras
\mathfrak{g}^{\Gamma_{1}}

and
\mathfrak{g}^{\Gamma_{2}}
that correspond to
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
. It is uniquely defined by the property

\gamma e_{\alpha}=e_{\gamma(\alpha)}
for
\alpha\in\Gamma_{1}
, where
e_{\alpha}
is the Chevalley generator corresponding to
the root
\alpha
. The isomorphism
\gamma^{*}{:\ }\mathfrak{g}^{\Gamma_{2}}\to\mathfrak{g}^{\Gamma_{1}}
is defined as the adjoint to
\gamma
with respect to the form
\langle{\cdot},{\cdot}\rangle
. It corresponds to the isometry
\gamma^{*}
and
is given by
\gamma^{*}e_{\gamma(\alpha)}=e_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\Gamma_{1}
.
Both
\gamma
and
\gamma^{*}
can be extended to maps of
\mathfrak{g}
to itself by applying first the orthogonal
projection on
\mathfrak{g}^{\Gamma_{1}}
(respectively, on
\mathfrak{g}^{\Gamma_{2}}
) with respect to
\langle{\cdot},{\cdot}\rangle
; clearly, the extended
maps remain adjoint to each other. Note that the restrictions of
\gamma
and
\gamma^{*}
to the positive and the negative nilpotent subalgebras
\mathfrak{n}_{+}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{-}
of
\mathfrak{g}
are Lie algebra homomorphisms
of
\mathfrak{n}_{+}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{-}
to themselves, and
\gamma(e_{\pm\alpha})=0

for all
\alpha\in\Pi\setminus\Gamma_{1}
. Further, if
\mathfrak{g}
is simply connected
\gamma
can be lifted to
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\exp\gamma
; note that
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
is defined only on subgroups
\mathcal{N}_{+}
and
\mathcal{N}_{-}
that correspond to
\mathfrak{n}_{+}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{-}
,
and is a group homomorphism.

By the classification theorem, each classical R-matrix is equivalent to an R-matrix
r^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
from a
Belavin–Drinfeld class
defined by a BD triple
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. The operator
R^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{+}
corresponding
to a member of this class is given by

(2.4)

R^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{+}=R_{0}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}+\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\pi_{>}-\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\pi_{<},

where
\pi_{>}
,
\pi_{<}
are projections of

\mathfrak{g}
onto
\mathfrak{n}_{+}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{-}
and
R_{0}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
acts on
\mathfrak{h}
(see
[
18
]
for more details).

2.2.
Generalized cluster structures and compatible Poisson brackets

Following
[
15
,
16
]
, we remind the definition of a generalized cluster structure represented by a quiver with multiplicities. Let
Q=(Q,d_{1},\dots,d_{N})
be
a quiver on
N
mutable and
M
frozen vertices with positive integer multiplicities
d_{i}
at mutable vertices.
A vertex is called
special
if its multiplicity is greater than 1.
A frozen vertex is called
isolated
if it is not connected to any other vertices.

An
extended seed
is a triple
\Sigma=({\bf x},Q,{\mathcal{P}})
, where
{\bf x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N},x_{N+1},\dots,\allowbreak x_{N+M})
are
cluster and frozen variables attached to the vertices of
Q

and
{\mathcal{P}}
is a set of
N

strings
. The
i
th string is a collection of
monomials
p_{ir}\in{\mathbb{A}}={\mathbb{Z}}[x_{N+1},\dots,x_{N+M}]
,

0\leq r\leq d_{i}
, such that

p_{i0}=p_{id_{i}}=1
; it is called
trivial
if
d_{i}=1
, and hence both elements of the string are equal to one.
The monomials
p_{ir}
are called
exchange coefficients
.

Given a seed as above, the
adjacent cluster
in direction
k
,
1\leq k\leq N
,
is defined by
{\bf x}^{\prime}=({\bf x}\setminus\{x_{k}\})\cup\{x^{\prime}_{k}\}
,
where the new cluster variable
x^{\prime}_{k}
is given by the
generalized exchange relation

(2.5)

x_{k}x^{\prime}_{k}=\sum_{r=0}^{d_{k}}p_{kr}u_{k;>}^{r}v_{k;>}^{[r]}u_{k;<}^{d_{k}-r}v_{k;<}^{[d_{k}-r]}

with

u_{k;>}=\prod_{k\to i\in Q}x_{i},\qquad u_{k;<}=\prod_{i\to k\in Q}x_{i},

and

v_{k;>}^{[s]}=\prod_{N+1\leq i\leq N+M}x_{i}^{\lfloor sb_{ki}/d_{k}\rfloor},\qquad v_{k;<}^{[s]}=\prod_{N+1\leq i\leq N+M}x_{i}^{\lfloor sb_{ik}/d_{k}\rfloor},

where
b_{ki}
is the number of edges from
k
to
i
and
b_{ik}
is the number of edges from
i
to
k
.
The strings remain unchanged, except for the
k
-th one that is reversed:
p^{\prime}_{kr}=p_{k,d_{k}-r}
.

The standard definition of the
quiver mutation
in direction
k
is modified as follows:
if both vertices
i
and
j

in a path
i\to k\to j
are mutable, then this path contributes
d_{k}
edges
i\to j
to the mutated quiver
Q^{\prime}
; if one of the vertices
i
or
j
is frozen then the path contributes
d_{j}
or
d_{i}
edges
i\to j
to
Q^{\prime}
. The multiplicities at the vertices do not change. Note that isolated vertices remain isolated in
Q^{\prime}
.

Given an extended seed
\Sigma=({\bf x},Q,{\mathcal{P}})
, we say that a seed

\Sigma^{\prime}=({\bf x}^{\prime},Q^{\prime},{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime})
is
adjacent
to
\Sigma
(in direction

k
) if
{\bf x}^{\prime}
,
Q^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
are as above.
Two such seeds are
mutation equivalent
if they can
be connected by a sequence of pairwise adjacent seeds.
The set of all seeds mutation equivalent to
\Sigma
is called the
generalized cluster structure
associated with
\Sigma
and denoted by
{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}={{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}(\Sigma)
.

Fix a ground ring
\widehat{{\mathbb{A}}}
such that
{\mathbb{A}}\subseteq\widehat{\mathbb{A}}\subseteq\bar{\mathbb{A}}={\mathbb{Z}}[x_{N+1}^{\pm 1},\dots,x_{N+M}^{\pm 1}]
. The
generalized upper cluster algebra

\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}({{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}})
is the intersection of the rings of Laurent polynomials over
\widehat{{\mathbb{A}}}
in cluster variables taken over all seeds in
{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}
. Let
V
be a rational variety over
{\mathbb{C}}
,
{\mathbb{C}}(V)
be the function field of
V
, and
{\mathcal{O}}(V)
be the ring of regular functions on
V
. A generalized cluster structure
{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}

in
{\mathbb{C}}(V)
is an embedding of
{\bf x}
into
{\mathbb{C}}(V)
that can be extended to a field isomorphism between
the ambient field tensored with
{\mathbb{C}}
and
{\mathbb{C}}(V)
.
It is called
regular on
V
if any cluster variable in any cluster belongs to
{\mathcal{O}}(V)
, and
complete
if

\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}({{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}})
tensored with
{\mathbb{C}}
is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{O}}(V)
. The choice of the ground ring is discussed
in
[
15
, Section 2.1]
.

Let
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
be a Poisson bracket on the ambient field, and
{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}
be a generalized cluster structure.
We say that the bracket and the generalized cluster structure are
compatible
if any extended
cluster
\widetilde{{\bf x}}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N+M})
is
log-canonical
with respect to
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
, that is,

\{x_{i},x_{j}\}=\omega_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}
,
where
\omega_{ij}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
are constants for all
i,j
,
1\leq i,j\leq N+M
. Instrumental in the study of compatibility
are certain Laurent monomials in cluster and frozen variables called
y
-variables
. They are defined for any mutable vertex
k\in Q
via

(2.6)

y_{k}=\frac{u_{k;>}v_{k;>}}{u_{k;<}v_{k;<}}.

2.3.
Regular pullbacks of a seed and almost-cluster structures

Let
\Psi:{\mathbb{A}}^{L^{\prime}}\to{\mathbb{A}}^{L}
be a dominant rational map. Assume that the algebra of regular functions on
{\mathbb{A}}^{L}
is endowed with a cluster structure
{\mathcal{C}}
defined by an
initial seed
\Sigma=({\bf x},Q)
. Let
x_{k}
be the cluster variable attached to a vertex
k
of
Q
; put
\tilde{x}_{k}=x_{k}\circ\Psi
. Clearly,
\tilde{x}_{k}
is a rational function on
{\mathbb{A}}^{L^{\prime}}
; let
u_{k}
and
z_{k}
be the numerator
and the denominator of
\tilde{x}_{k}
, assuming they are coprime. Further, let
\{w_{p}\}_{p=1}^{P}
be the family of all irreducible factors of the denominators
z_{k}
over all vertices of
Q
, so that each
z_{k}
can be written
as
z_{k}=\prod_{p=1}^{P}w_{p}^{\lambda_{pk}}
for some nonnegative integer
\lambda_{pk}
; the polynomials
w_{p}
are called

distinguished
.
The
discrepancy
at vertex
k

relative to
w_{p}
is defined via

(2.7)

\delta_{pk}=\sum_{i}b_{ik}\lambda_{pi}-\sum_{j}b_{kj}\lambda_{pj}.

Define a quiver
\Psi^{*}Q
as a copy of
Q
with
P
additional frozen vertices corresponding to
w_{p}
,
p\in[1,P]
. There are
|\delta_{pk}|
arrows between the frozen vertex
p
and the vertex
k
in
\Psi^{*}Q
; they are directed from
k
to
p
if
\delta_{pk}<0
, and in the opposite direction otherwise.
We thus get a seed
\Psi^{*}\Sigma=({\bf u},\Psi^{*}Q)
by attaching
u_{k}
to the vertex
k
and
w_{p}
to the frozen
vertex
p
. This seed is called the
regular pullback
of
\Sigma
. Depending on the choice of the ground ring,
it defines a cluster structure on
{\mathbb{A}}^{L^{\prime}}
or on a quasi-affine variety
U\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{L^{\prime}}
given by non-vanishing of some of
w_{p}
.

The following fact is a direct consequence the definition.

Proposition 2.1
.

The
y
-variables for the seed
\Psi^{*}\Sigma
coincide with the pullback by
\Psi
of
y
-variables
for the seed
\Sigma
.

Note that in general pullbacks and mutations of seeds do not commute. If they do, the pullback is called coherent. Sufficient conditions for the existence of a coherent regular pullback are given in
[
20
]
.

The construction of the regular pullback can be extended to the case of generalized cluster structures. In this case the quiver
\Psi^{*}Q
may have 2-cycles of a special type, namely, opposite arrows between a vertex of multiplicity greater than one and a frozen vertex. Expression (
2.7
) for the discrepancy at vertices of multiplicity
d_{k}>1
is modified by multiplying
the contributions of mutable neighbors by
d_{k}
and retaining the contributions of frozen neighbors. In the contrast to the case of a regular pullback of a cluster structure, the discrepancy
\delta_{pk}
defines only the difference between the number of arrows between
k
and
p
in two opposite directions. To find the actual number of arrows, the additional information is needed.
This additional information consists of a function
\chi_{pk}(r)
,
r\in[0,d_{k}]
, and a slope
\tau_{pk}
that are defined
via the degrees of polynomials
w_{p}
in the various terms of the generalized exchange relation (
2.5
),
see
[
20
, Section 4]
for details. If the coherency conditions are satisfied, the seed
\Psi^{*}\Sigma=({\bf u},\Psi^{*}Q,\Psi^{*}{\mathcal{P}})
defines an
almost-cluster
structure. The first and the last terms of exchange relations in
such structures do not need to be coprime, since they might involve certain powers of the same frozen variable.
The upper cluster algebra for an almost-cluster structure is defined, similarly to the case of cluster structures, as the intersection of the rings of Laurent polynomials over the ring of polynomials in frozen variables taken over all seeds.

A similar situation occurs when a regular generalized cluster structure on a quasi-affine variety
V
is lifted to the ambient affine space
{\mathbb{A}}^{L}
. In this case the role of distinguished polynomials
w_{p}
is played by irreducible polynomials that define the divisors whose union is the complement of
V
in
{\mathbb{A}}^{L}
. In
[
20
, Theorem 6.2]
we give sufficient conditions for a regular complete generalized cluster structure on
V

to have a coherent complete regular pullback to
{\mathbb{A}}^{L}
.

2.4.
Main result

The study of cluster structures on
{\mathcal{G}}
compatible with the bracket (
2.3
) for
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{n}

in
[
18
,
19
]
revealed that there are two essentially different types of pairs of BD triples

(\mathbf{\Gamma},\bar{\mathbf{\Gamma}})
.
The condition distinguishing these two types can be formulated in terms of
the map
\delta=(-w_{0})\bar{\gamma}^{*}(-w_{0})\gamma
: we say that a pair
(\mathbf{\Gamma},\bar{\mathbf{\Gamma}})
violates the aperiodicity
condition if there exists
\alpha\in\Gamma_{1}
and natural
m>0
such that
\delta^{m}(\alpha)=\alpha
;
otherwise the pair
(\mathbf{\Gamma},\bar{\mathbf{\Gamma}})
is called
aperiodic
(see

[
18
, Section 3.1]
for a more detailed discussion of these two types in terms of acyclicity
of corresponding BD graphs). Papers
[
18
,
19
]
provide a comprehensive study of cluster structures on
SL_{n}
compatible with aperiodic pairs of BD triples
The case of pairs that violate the aperiodicity condition is much more complicated and leads to
generalized cluster structures. The simplest way to get such a pair
(\mathbf{\Gamma},\bar{\mathbf{\Gamma}})
is to choose

\bar{\mathbf{\Gamma}}=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{w_{0}}
. In the self-dual case
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{w_{0}}
this produces generalised cluster structures
compatible with the Poisson–Lie structure. The smallest case of this type occurs for
n=6
and is considered
in detail in
[
17
]
.

In this paper we study the pair
(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{w_{0}})
for the Cremmer–Gervais BD triple
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{{\rm CG}}
. Namely,
let
\Pi=\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n-1}\}
be the set of positive simple roots for the
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
-case; the Cremmer–Gervais triple is given by
\Gamma_{1}=\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n-2}\}
,

\Gamma_{2}=\{\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{n-1}\}
, and
\gamma{:\ }\alpha_{i}\mapsto\alpha_{i+1}
for
i\in[1,n-2]
. Note that in this
case
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{w_{0}}=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{*}
. Recall that the study of the aperiodic pair
(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\rm CG},\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\rm CG})
for the Cremmer–Gervais triple provided one of the first examples of exotic cluster structures on
GL_{n}
, see
[
13
,
14
]
.
Let
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
denote the bracket (
2.3
) with
r=r^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
and
\bar{r}=r^{\mathbf{\Gamma}^{w_{0}}}
for
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\rm CG}
. We retain the same notation for the lift of this bracket to a Poisson bracket on
GL_{n}
for which
\det X

is a Casimir. The main result of the paper is

Theorem 2.2
.

For any
n\geq 4
, there exists a regular complete generalized cluster structure
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
on
GL_{n}
compatible with
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
.

Remark 2.3
.

The ground ring for the generalized cluster structure mentioned in the Theorem is
{\mathbb{C}}[x_{1n},x_{n1},\det X^{\pm 1}]
. As usual, the same construction (with evident minor modifications) provides a regular complete
generalized structure on
SL_{n}
over the ground ring
{\mathbb{C}}[x_{1n},x_{n_{1}}]
. Extending this structure to

\operatorname{Mat}_{n}
(over the ground ring
{\mathbb{C}}[x_{1n},x_{n1},\det X]
) is somewhat more subtle and will be adressed below in
Section
2.7
.6.

Remark 2.4
.

Theorem
2.2
remains valid for
n=3
as well.
The case
n=3
is special, and certain constructions that work for
n\geq 4
should be modified. It is discussed in detail in a companion paper
[
20
]
.

In fact, for
n\geq 4
, we construct
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
as an amalgamation of the two cluster structures closely related to the two previously known ones: the generalized cluster
structure on
GL_{n-1}^{\dagger}
compatible with the bracket (
2.2
) for
r=r^{\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\rm CG}}

studied in
[
21
]
and
the cluster structure on rational functions studied in
[
11
]
. To make the exposition self-contained, we provide below a concise description of these structures.

2.5.
Generalized cluster structure on
GL_{n}^{\dagger}
compatible with the Cremmer–Gervais bracket

A regular complete generalized cluster structure on
GL_{n}^{\dagger}
compatible with the the bracket (
2.2
) for arbitrary BD data was built in
[
21
]
. In this paper, we need a specialization of this construction for the Cremmer–Gervais BD data. Denote by
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{\dagger}
the bracket (
2.2
) for
r=r^{\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\rm CG}}
. The generalized structure
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}^{\dagger}_{n}

on
GL_{n}^{\dagger}
built in
[
21
]
is defined by the quiver
Q_{n}^{\dagger}
with one special vertex of multiplicity
n
, initial extended cluster
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
and a unique coefficient string
P_{n}
.

The quiver
Q_{n}^{\dagger}
for
n\geq 3
consists of two equilateral
triangles each comprised of
n(n-1)/2
vertices. The triangles are placed base to base making a lozenge, see Fig.
1
for the case
n=5
; for the sake of visualization we call them the left triangle and the right triangle. The leftmost vertex of the lozenge is the special vertex, the rightmost one is the frozen vertex, all other vertices are mutable. The vertices in the left triangle are labeled by pairs
(k,l)
with
1\leq k,l\leq n-1
,
k+l\leq n
, the leftmost vertex being
(1,1)
,
k
increasing along the upper side of
the triangle and
l
increasing along its lower side, so that the vertices of the base traversed bottom up are
(m,n-m)
for

1\leq m\leq n-1
. The vertices in the right triangle are labeled by pairs
\langle i,j\rangle=\langle n+1-k,n+2-k-l\rangle
with

1\leq k,l\leq n-1
,
k+l\leq n
, the rightmost vertex
being
\langle n,n\rangle
and corresponding to
k=l=1
,
k
increasing along the lower side of the triangle and
l
increasing along its upper side, so that the vertices of the base traversed bottom up are
\langle m,2\rangle
for
2\leq m\leq n
. This numbering is inherited from the numbering of the vertices of the quiver for the generalized cluster structure on the Drinfeld double of

GL_{n}
derived in
[
15
]
, which accounts for the difference in the numberings in the left and right triangles.

The arrows in both triangles form a complete triangular mesh with arrows directed south, northeast, and northwest,
with the omission of arrows along the upper side of the left triangle and the lower side of the right triangle. The two
bases are connected with the arrows
\langle m,2\rangle\to(m-1,n-m+1)
for
2\leq m\leq n
directed west and
(m-1,n-m+1)\to\langle m+1,2\rangle

for
2\leq m\leq n-1
directed northeast. Additionally, there is a path of length
2(n-2)
formed by arrows

(1,l)\to(l+1,1)\to(1,l+1)
for
1\leq l\leq n-2
in the left triangle and a path of the same length formed by arrows

\langle n,j-1\rangle\to\langle j-1,j-1\rangle\to\langle n,j\rangle
for
1\leq j\leq n-2
in the right triangle. For the sake of better visualization, these two paths are shown with dashed lines
in Fig.
1
. Finally, there are an additional frozen vertex
(0,0)
,
n-1
isolated frozen vertices that are not shown
in Fig.
1
, and two additional arrows: from
(1,1)
in the left triangle to the additional frozen vertex
(0,0)
and from
this vertex to
\langle 2,2\rangle
in the right triangle.

Figure 1.
Quiver
Q_{5}^{\dagger}

Following
[
21
, Section 7.2]
, the initial extended cluster
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
consists of three subfamilies of functions.
Let
U
be an element of
GL_{n}^{\dagger}
.
Define
n\times n
matrices
\Phi_{kl}(U)
,
k,l\in[1,n-1]
,
k+l\leq n
, by taking the first
k
columns of
U^{0}
, appending the first
l
columns of
U^{1}=U
, and then appending the first column of the matrices
U^{p}
for
2\leq p\leq n-k-l+1
. The first subfamily consists of
n(n-1)/2
functions
\varphi_{kl}(U)
attached to the vertices
(k,l)
of the left triangle and defined
via

(2.8)

\varphi_{kl}(U)=s_{kl}^{n}\det\Phi_{kl}(U),

where the sign
s_{kl}^{n}
is given by

(2.9)

s_{kl}^{n}=\begin{cases}(-1)^{k(l+1)}\ &\ \text{for $n$ even},\\
(-1)^{(n-1)/2+k(k-1)/2+l(l-1)/2}\ &\ \text{for $n$ odd}.\end{cases}

The second subfamily consists of
n(n-1)/2
functions
g_{ij}(U)
attached to the vertices
\langle i,j\rangle
of the right triangle
and defined via
g_{i2}(U)=\det U_{[i,n]}^{[2,n-i+2]}
and

(2.10)

g_{ij}(U)=\sum_{I_{1},I_{2},\dots,I_{j-2}}\det U^{I_{1}}_{[i,n]}\det U_{\gamma^{*}(I_{1})\cup[n-i+j,n]}^{I_{2}}\prod_{m=2}^{j-2}\det U_{\gamma^{*}(I_{m})\cup n}^{I_{m+1}}

for
j>2
, where the sum is taken over all subsets
I_{1},\dots,I_{j-2}
of
I_{j-1}=[2,n]
of sizes
|I_{1}|=n-i+1
and
|I_{m}|=n-j+m
for

2\leq m\leq j-2
(see Section
4.2
below for the derivation of the above expression from the general procedure
for constructing functions
g_{ij}(U)
for an arbitrary BD data presented in
[
21
]
).
The third subfamily consists
of
n
functions
c_{r}(U)
,
1\leq r\leq n
, that are just signed coefficients of the characteristic polynomial of
U
:

(2.11)

\det(\mathbf{1}_{n}+\lambda U)=1+\sum_{r=1}^{n}(-1)^{r(n-1)}\lambda^{r}c_{r}(U).

Functions
c_{r}(U)
for
1\leq r\leq n-1
are attached to the isolated vertices,
c_{n}(U)=\det U
is attached to the
additional frozen vertex
(0,0)
. The only string in
{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
is
(1,c_{1}(U),\allowbreak\dots,c_{n-1}(U),1)
, so that the exchange
relation at the special vertex for the seed
({\mathcal{D}}_{n},Q_{n}^{\dagger},{\mathcal{P}}_{n})
reads

\varphi_{11}(U)\varphi_{11}^{\prime}(U)=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\varphi_{21}^{r}(U)\varphi_{12}^{n-r}(U)c_{r}(U)

with
c_{0}(U)=1
.

The following theorem is proved in
[
21
]
.

Theorem 2.5
.

For any
n\geq 3
, the initial seed
({\mathcal{D}}_{n},Q_{n}^{\dagger},{\mathcal{P}}_{n})
defines a regular complete generalized cluster structure

{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n}^{\dagger}
on
GL_{n}
compatible with the bracket
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{\dagger}
.

Remark 2.6
.

Note that the ground ring for the corresponding generalized upper cluster algebra
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}({{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n}^{\dagger})
is

{\mathbb{C}}[g_{nn},\det U^{\pm 1}]
.

Clearly, a (generalized) cluster structure compatible with a Poisson bracket is also compatible with its negative, therefore
{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n}^{\dagger}
is compatible with the bracket
-{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{\dagger}
as well.

2.6.
Cluster structure on rational functions

Denote by
{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
the space of complex rational functions of one
variable of the form

M(\lambda)=q(\lambda)/p(\lambda)
, where
p(\lambda)
is a monic polynomial of degree
n\geq 2
and
q(\lambda)
is a polynomial
of degree at most
n-1
coprime with
p(\lambda)
. The cluster structure
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{T}
on
{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
compatible with the bracket

(2.12)

\{M(\lambda),M(\mu)\}^{T}=-(\lambda M(\lambda)-\mu M(\mu))\frac{M(\lambda)-M(\mu)}{\lambda-\mu}

is built in
[
11
]
. Below we describe a slight modification of this construction related to the initial one via
a quasi-isomorphism. The quiver
Q^{T}_{n}
consists of two rows of
n
vertices each. The vertices in the upper and lower rows are
labeled from right to left by
(m,+)
and
(m,-)
,
1\leq m\leq n
, respectively. The leftmost vertices in each row,
(n,+)

and
(n,-)
, are frozen, all other vertices are mutable. There are horizontal arrows
(m,+)\to(m-1,+)

and
(m,-)\to(m-1,-)

for
2\leq m\leq n
, double vertical arrows
(m,-)\Rightarrow(m,+)
,
1\leq m\leq n-1
, directed upwards, double inclined arrows
(m,+)\Rightarrow(m+1,-)
,
1\leq m\leq n-2
, directed downwards, and an ordinary
inclined arrow
(n-1,+)\to(n,-)
directed downwards, see Fig.
2
.

Figure 2.
Quiver
Q_{5}^{T}

To build the initial extended cluster
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}
,
write the Laurent expansion

M(\lambda)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{h_{i}}{\lambda^{i+1}}

and define for each
m\in[1,n]
two
m\times m
Hankel matrices
H_{m}^{-}=(h_{\alpha+\beta-2})_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{m}
and

H_{m}^{+}=(h_{\alpha+\beta-1})_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{m}
. The family
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}
consists of
2n-1
functions
t_{m}^{-}=\det H_{m}^{-}
,
1\leq m\leq n
, and

t_{m}^{+}=\det H_{m}^{+}
,
1\leq m\leq n-1
, assigned to vertices
(m,-)
and
(m,+)
, respectively, and the
function
t_{n}^{+}/t_{n}^{-}
assigned to vertex
(n,+)
. Note that the bracket (
2.12
) in terms of
the coefficients
h_{i}
reads

(2.13)

\{h_{i},h_{j}\}^{T}=\sum_{k=i}^{j-1}h_{k+1}h_{i+j-k-1}

for
i<j
.

Remark 2.7
.

1. The quiver described above should be compared to the quiver (5.28) in
[
11
]

reflected about its center. The frozen variable attached to the frozen vertex
(n,+)
differs from the one attached to the corresponding frozen vertex in the quiver (5.28), which is
equal to
t_{n}^{-}/t_{n}^{+}
. This difference accounts for the reversal of the horizontal arrow

(n,+)\to(n-1,+)
.

2. The definition of the space
{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
in
[
11
]
contains an additional technical requirement
p(0)\neq 0
, which is not needed in our situation. In
[
11
]
, where the cluster structure on
{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
was used to give a cluster theory interpretation to Bäcklund–Darboux transformations between Coxeter–Toda flows on
GL_{n}
, it was used to distinguish
rational functions that are scalar multiples of Weyl functions of elements of Coxeter double Bruhat cells.

3. Expression (
2.13
) for the bracket is borrowed from
[
6
]
;
a misprint in the upper summation limit is corrected.

The construction presented above can be easily extended to the space
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
of complex rational functions of one
variable of the form
\bar{M}(\lambda)=\bar{q}(\lambda)/p(\lambda)
, where
p(\lambda)
is the same as before and
\bar{q}(\lambda)
is a polynomial of degree at most
n
coprime with
p(\lambda)
. The Laurent expansion for
\bar{M}(\lambda)
reads

(2.14)

\bar{M}(\lambda)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{\bar{h}_{i}}{\lambda^{i}}.

Clearly, for
i>0
coefficients
\bar{h}_{i}
coincide with
h_{i-1}
computed for the function
M(\lambda)=\bar{M}(\lambda)-\bar{h}_{0}\in{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
. We extend the bracket
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{T}
to
\bar{h}_{i}
via

(2.15)

\{\bar{h}_{i},\bar{h}_{j}\}^{T}=\sum_{k=i}^{j-1}\bar{h}_{k+1}\bar{h}_{i+j-k-1}

for
i<j
, which immediately yields

(2.16)

\{\bar{M}(\lambda),\bar{M}(\mu)\}^{T}=-(\lambda\bar{M}(\mu)-\mu\bar{M}(\lambda))\frac{\bar{M}(\lambda)-\bar{M}(\mu)}{\lambda-\mu}.

The quiver
\bar{Q}_{n}^{T}
contains
n
vertices
(m,+)
,
1\leq m\leq n
, and
n+1
vertices
(m,-)
,
1\leq m\leq n+1
. The two frozen vertices are
(n,+)
and
(n+1,-)
. All arrows of the quiver
Q_{n}^{T}
are present in

\bar{Q}_{n}^{T}
, except for the inclined arrow
(n-1,+)\to(n,-)
which is replaced by a double inclined arrow

(n-1,+)\Rightarrow(n,-)
. Additionally, there is a vertical arrow
(n,-)\to(n,+)
and a horizontal arrow

(n+1,-)\to(n,-)
. We draw
\bar{Q}_{n}^{T}
in a lozenge-like way, see Fig.
3
.

Figure 3.
Quiver
\bar{Q}_{5}^{T}

Similarly to above, we define Hankel matrices
\bar{H}_{m}^{-}=(\bar{h}_{\alpha+\beta-2})_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{m}
and

\bar{H}_{m}^{+}=(\bar{h}_{\alpha+\beta-1})_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{m}
and the corresponding determinants
\bar{t}_{m}^{-}=\det\bar{H}_{m}^{-}
,
m\in[1,n+1]
, and
\bar{t}_{m}^{+}=(-1)^{m}\det\bar{H}_{m}^{+}
,
m\in[1,n]
. The initial cluster
\bar{\mathcal{T}}_{n}
consists of
2n
functions
\bar{t}_{m}^{\pm}
,

1\leq m\leq n
, assigned to vertices
(m,+)
and
(m,-)
, respectively, and the function

\bar{t}_{n+1}^{-}/\bar{t}_{n}^{+}
assigned to vertex
(n+1,-)
.

Theorem 2.8
.

For any
n\geq 2
, the initial seed
(\bar{\mathcal{T}}_{n},\bar{Q}_{n}^{T})
defines a regular complete cluster structure
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{T}
on

\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
compatible with the bracket
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{T}
.

Remark 2.9
.

Note that the ground ring for the corresponding upper cluster algebra
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}(\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{T})
is

{\mathbb{C}}[\bar{t}_{n+1}^{-}/\bar{t}_{n}^{+},(\bar{t}_{n}^{+})^{\pm 1}]
.

The proof of this theorem is given in the Appendix.

2.7.
Outline of the proof

In what follows in this section we assume that
n\geq 4
, unless explicitly stated otherwise. To prove Theorem
2.2
we perform the following steps.

1. We build two maps:
\Psi^{\prime}:GL_{n}\to GL_{n-1}
and
\Psi^{\prime\prime}:GL_{n}\to\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{n-1}
and prove the following theorem.

Theorem 2.10
.

(i)
The map
\Psi^{\prime}:(GL_{n},{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}})\to\widetilde{GL}_{n-1}^{{\dagger}}=(GL_{n-1},-{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{\dagger})
is Poisson.

(ii)
The map
\Psi^{\prime\prime}:(GL_{n},{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}})\to\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{n-1}^{T}=(\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{n-1},{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{T})
is Poisson.

(iii)
The combined map
\Psi:(GL_{n},{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}})\to\widetilde{GL}_{n-1}^{{\dagger}}\times_{\mathbb{C}}\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{n-1}^{T}
with the fibre product defined by functions
\det U
for
U\in GL_{n-1}
and
\bar{q}(0)
for
\bar{q}(\lambda)/p(\lambda)\in\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{n-1}
is Poisson.

2. We consider the cluster structures
{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n-1}^{\dagger}
and
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}^{T}
described in Sections
2.5
and
2.6
, respectively. The corresponding quivers are shown schematically
in Fig.
4
.

Figure 4.
Quivers
Q_{n-1}^{\dagger}
(on the left) and
\bar{Q}_{n-1}^{T}
(on the right)

To find the functions
\varphi_{kl}(\Psi^{\prime}(X))
,
g_{ij}(\Psi^{\prime}(X))
,
c_{p}(\Psi^{\prime}(X))
,
and
\bar{t}_{m}^{\pm}(\Psi^{\prime\prime}(X)
for
X\in GL_{n}
we need the following objects.

First, we define
(n-1)(n-2)/2
matrices
{\mathsf{\Phi}}_{kl}(X)
for
k,l\in[1,n-2]
and
k+l\leq n-1

of sizes
(n-k-l)(n+1)\times(n-k-l)(n+1)
. Next, we define
(n-1)(n-2)/2
matrices
{\tt G}_{ij}(X)

for
i\in[2,n-1]
and
j\in[2,i]
of sizes
(n+j-1)\times(n+j-1)
. Finally, we define three more matrices:

{\tt A}(X)
and
{\tt B}(X)
of size
(n+1)\times(n+1)
and
{\tt F}(X)
of size
(2n-1)\times(2n-1)
. The entries of all the
matrices are either zeros or entries of
X
. Explicit constructions of the matrices are given in Section
4
below.

Put
\phi_{kl}(X)=\det{\mathsf{\Phi}}_{kl}(X)
,
{\tt g}_{ij}(X)=\det{\tt G}_{ij}(X)
, and define
{\tt c}_{p}(X)
as the
coefficient at
\lambda^{p}
of
\det({\tt A}(X)+\lambda{\tt B}(X))
with the sign
(-1)^{pn}
and
{\tt f}_{m}(X)
as the
m
-th main trailing minor of
{\tt F}(X)
starting from the lower right corner. It follows from above that all these functions are polynomials in the entries of
X
.

Theorem 2.11
.

(i)
Functions
\varphi_{kl}(\Psi^{\prime}(X))
for
k,l\in[1,n-2]
and
k+l\leq n-1
can be written as

(2.17)

\varphi_{kl}(\Psi^{\prime}(X))={\tt s}_{kl}\frac{\phi_{kl}(X)}{(x_{1n}\det X)^{n-k-l}}

with

{\tt s}_{kl}=\begin{cases}(-1)^{(n-2)/2+k(k-1)/2+l(l-1)/2}\ &\ \text{for $n$ even},\\
(-1)^{(n-1)/2+k(k-1)/2+l(l-1)/2}\ &\ \text{for $n$ odd}.\end{cases}

(ii)
Functions
g_{ij}(\Psi^{\prime}(X))
for
i\in[2,n-1]
and
j\in[2,i]
can be written as

(2.18)

g_{ij}(\Psi^{\prime}(X))=\hat{\tt s}_{ij}\frac{{\tt g}_{ij}(X)}{x_{1n}^{j-1}\det X}

with
\hat{\tt s}_{ij}=(-1)^{(n+j+1)(i+j)}
.

(iii)
Functions
c_{r}(\Psi^{\prime}(X))
can be written as

(2.19)

c_{r}(\Psi^{\prime}(X))=\frac{{\tt c}_{r}(X)}{x_{1n}\det X}

for
r\in[1,n-1]
. In particular,
c_{n-1}(\Psi^{\prime}(X))=\det\Psi^{\prime}(X)=x_{n1}/x_{1n}
.

(iv)
Functions
\bar{t}_{m}^{\pm}(\Psi^{\prime\prime}(X))
can be written as

(2.20)

\bar{t}_{m}^{-}(\Psi^{\prime\prime}(X))=\frac{{\tt f}_{2m-1}(X)}{x_{1n}^{2m-1}},\qquad\bar{t}_{m}^{+}(\Psi^{\prime\prime}(X))=\frac{{\tt f}_{2m}(X)}{x_{1n}^{2m}},

where
m\in[1,n]
in the first case and
m\in[1,n-1]
in the second case.
In particular,
\bar{t}_{n}^{-}(\Psi^{\prime\prime}(X))/\bar{t}_{n-1}^{+}(\Psi^{\prime\prime}(X))=x_{n1}/x_{1n}
.

Remark 2.12
.

Note that the map
\Psi
is defined for
n=3
as well, and Theorem
2.11
remains valid
in this case.

The following fact is checked via a straightforward calculation at each vertex.

Corollary 2.13
.

For any mutable vertex
v
of
Q_{n-1}^{\dagger}
, the product of the signs
{\tt s}_{kl}
and
\hat{\tt s}_{ij}
over all
vertices
(k,l)
and
\langle i,j\rangle
adjacent to
v
equals one.

Consequently, we can modify
{\mathcal{D}}_{n-1}
via replacing each
\varphi_{kl}
by
{\tt s}_{kl}\varphi_{kl}
and each
g_{ij}
by

\hat{\tt s}_{ij}g_{ij}
, and Theorem
2.5
remains valid for
the modified cluster
\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}_{n-1}
.

3. We build regular pullbacks of the seeds
(\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}_{n-1},Q_{n-1}^{\dagger},{\mathcal{P}}_{n-1})
and
(\bar{\mathcal{T}}_{n-1},\bar{Q}_{n-1}^{T})
under
\Psi^{\prime}
and
\Psi^{\prime\prime}
, respectively, based on
explicit expressions given in Theorem
2.11
and the constructions described in Section
2.3
.

Proposition 2.14
.

(i)
The quiver
\Psi^{\prime*}Q_{n-1}^{\dagger}
is obtained from
Q_{n-1}^{\dagger}
by adding two frozen vertices
A
and
B
and four arrows:
(n-2,1)\to A\to(1,1)
and
(n-2,1)\to B\to\langle n-1,2\rangle
.

(ii)
The quiver
\Psi^{\prime\prime*}\bar{Q}_{n-1}^{T}
is obtained from
\bar{Q}_{n-1}^{T}
by adding an additional frozen vertex
A
and an arrow
A\to(1,-)
.

The corresponding quivers are shown schematically in Fig.
5
. The vertices of the quivers

\Psi^{\prime*}Q_{n-1}^{\dagger}
and
\Psi^{\prime\prime*}\bar{Q}_{n-1}^{T}
retain the same labeling as in
Q_{n-1}^{\dagger}
and
\bar{Q}_{n-1}^{T}
, respectively. Cluster variables assigned to the vertices of
\Psi^{\prime*}Q_{n-1}^{\dagger}
are
\phi_{kl}
and
{\tt g}_{ij}
, those assigned to the vertices of
\Psi^{\prime\prime*}\bar{Q}_{n-1}^{T}
are
{\tt f}_{m}
. Note that since we consider the pullback to
GL_{n}
, the coefficients of the generalized exchange relation at
(1,1)
are
{\tt c}_{r}(X)/\det X
.

Figure 5.
Quivers
\Psi^{\prime*}Q_{n-1}^{\dagger}
(on the left) and
\Psi^{\prime\prime*}\bar{Q}_{n-1}^{T}
(on the right)

4. Observe that the same functions are assigned to vertices in both quivers marked by the same letters:

x_{1n}
to vertex
A
,
x_{n1}
to vertex
C
and
{\tt g}_{n-1,n-1}(X)={\tt f}_{2n-2}(X)
to vertex
D
;
to justify the latter claim we note that
{\tt G}_{n-1,n-1}(X)={\tt F}(X)_{[2,2n-1]}^{[2,2n-1]}
(see the definitions of these matrices in Section
4
below).
Therefore, we can glue the two quivers in Fig.
5

into one quiver via identifying these three pairs of frozen vertices.

Further, frozen vertices in a regular complete generalized cluster structure
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}
compatible with a Poisson bracket

{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}_{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}
are characterized by the following property (see Section
5.2
for the proof).

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}
be a regular complete generalized cluster structure on a variety
V
compatible with a Poisson bracket

{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}_{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}
. A cluster variable
f
in
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}
is frozen if and only if
\tilde{g}=\{f,g\}_{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}/f
is regular
for any regular function
g
.

Guided by Proposition
2.15
, we check that its condition holds for the variables
x_{1n}
and
x_{n1}
, see Section
5.2
below. However, we observe (by using computer-assisted computations) that for
n=4
,
\{{\tt f}_{6}(X),x_{23}\}/{\tt f}_{6}(X)
is not a regular function.
This suggests that while
x_{1n}
,
x_{n1}
, and
\det X
should remain frozen (recall that by construction
\det X
is a Casimir of the bracket
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
on
GL_{n}
),
one cannot get a regular complete generalized cluster structure without unfreezing vertex
D
.
Indeed, this fact is proved later for arbitrary
n
in Section
7
, see Remark
7.16
.
In order to get such a structure, we unfreeze vertex
D
. But then the degrees of the monomials in the would be
exchange relation are out of balance. To fix the imbalance, we have to add arrows to and/or from
D
. Clearly, an arrow between
D
and a mutable vertex would break the balance at this vertex, so the only possible new arrows are between
D
and other frozen vertices, that is,
A
,
B
, and
C
. It is easy to see that there exists at most one choice of additional arrows that guarantees that after the mutation at
D
the new function is regular. The following statement proven in Section
5.3
dictates this choice.

Proposition 2.16
.

Let
{\tt g}(X)=\det{\tt G}_{n-1,n-2}(X)_{[2,2n-3]}^{[2,2n-3]}
, then

(2.21)

{\tt g}_{n-1,n-1}(X){\tt g}(X)={\tt f}_{2n-3}(X){\tt g}_{n-2,n-2}(X)+x_{n1}{\tt f}_{2n-4}(X){\tt g}_{n-1,n-2}(X).

Consequently, we connect
D
with an additional arrow to vertex
C
.
The corresponding quiver
Q_{n}
is shown schematically in
Fig.
6
.

The initial cluster
{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
is defined as follows: functions
\phi_{kl}(X)
are assigned
to vertices
(k,l)
, functions
{\tt g}_{ij}(X)
are assigned to vertices
\langle i,j\rangle
, functions
{\tt f}_{2i}(X)

are assigned to vertices
(i,+)
, functions
{\tt f}_{2i-1}(X)
are assigned to vertices
(i,-1)
. Additionally,
{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
contains frozen variables
x_{1n}
assigned to
A
,
\det X
assigned to
B
and
x_{n1}
assigned to
C
.
Finally, there are isolated frozen variables
{\tt c}_{r}(X)
that enter the only string
{\mathcal{P}}_{n}=(1,{\tt c}_{1}(X)/\det X,\dots,{\tt c}_{n-2}(X)/\det X,1)
, so that the generalized exchange relation reads

(2.22)

\phi_{11}(X)\phi^{\prime}_{11}(X)=x_{1n}\phi_{12}^{n-1}(X)+\sum_{r=1}^{n-2}\frac{{\tt c}_{r}(X)}{\det X}\phi_{21}^{r}(X)\phi_{12}^{n-1-r}(X)+x_{n1}\phi_{21}^{n-1}(X).

Figure 6.
Quiver
Q_{n}
:

x_{1n}
assigned to
A
,
\det X
assigned to
B
,
x_{n1}
assigned to
C
,
{\tt g}_{n-1,n-1}(X)={\tt f}_{2n-2}(X)
assigned to
D

We can now reformulate Theorem
2.2
as follows.

Theorem 2.17
.

The generalized cluster structure
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}_{n}={\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{F}}_{n},Q_{n},{\mathcal{P}}_{n})
is a regular complete generalized cluster structure on
GL_{n}
compatible with
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
.

Remark 2.18
.

An analog of Theorem
2.17
is valid for
n=3
. In fact, the components
{\mathcal{F}}_{3}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
of the initial seed are defined by the same formulas as above; the quiver
Q_{3}

is different, see
[
20
]
for details.

5. For a diagonal matrix
D=\operatorname{diag}(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})
denote by
t^{D}
the diagonal matrix
\operatorname{diag}(t^{d_{1}},\dots,t^{d_{n}})
.
Let
D_{n}=\operatorname{diag}(n,\dots,2,1)
and
\bar{D}_{n}=\operatorname{diag}(1,2,\dots,n)
.
We say that a function
g
is homogeneous with respect to the action
X\mapsto t^{D_{n}}Xs^{\bar{D}_{n}}
if

(2.23)

g(t^{D_{n}}Xs^{\bar{D}_{n}})=t^{\xi_{g}}s^{\bar{\xi}_{g}}g(X),

where numbers
\xi_{g}
and
\bar{\xi}_{g}
depend only on
g
.

The proof of the compatibility statement in Theorem
2.17
is based
on
[
16
, Proposition 2.1]
and employs homogeneity of the cluster variables in the initial cluster

{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
, see Section
6
below for details. As an immediate corollary we get that the extended exchange matrix has a full rank (see
[
15
, Proposition 2.5]
).

Another corollary is the following statement.

Proposition 2.19
.

Every cluster variable in
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
is a homogeneous function in matrix entries of
X
and is also homogeneous with respect to the action
X\mapsto t^{D_{n}}Xs^{\bar{D}_{n}}
.

Remark 2.20
.

In fact, Proposition
2.19
describes the global toric action of
({\mathbb{C}}^{*})^{2}
on
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
,
see
[
15
, Section 2.1]
for relevant definitions. In line with the general conjecture, the rank of this action equals
|\Pi\setminus\Gamma_{1}|+|\Pi\setminus\bar{\Gamma}_{1}|=2
.

To proceed with the proof we invoke the following modification of
[
14
, Proposition 2.1]
.

Proposition 2.21
.

Let
V
be a Zariski open subset in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+m}
and
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}
be a generalized cluster structure in
{\mathbb{C}}(V)
with

n
mutable and
m
frozen variables such that

(i)
the initial extended cluster consists of regular functions;

(ii)
every mutation of the initial seed produces a regular function;

(iii)
every regular function on
V
belongs to the upper cluster algebra
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}})

Then
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}
is a regular complete generalized cluster structure.

Condition (i) follows from the construction above. Condition (ii) for all mutable vertices of
Q_{n}
distinct from vertex
D
follows immediately from Theorems
2.5
and
2.8
. Condition (ii) at vertex
D
is
verified by Proposition
2.16
.

To check condition (iii) in Proposition
2.21
, we use the following easy modification
of
[
14
, Lemma 8.3]
.

Proposition 2.22
.

Let
R\subset{\mathbb{C}}(V)
be a unique factorization domain and
F\in{\mathbb{C}}(V)
.
If there exist two Laurent representations
F=F_{1}/f_{1}=F_{2}/f_{2}
such that
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
belong to the upper cluster algebra
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}(V)
over
R
and
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are monomials in cluster variables that are coprime in
{\mathbb{C}}[V]
,
then
F
itself belongs to
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

We start with the following statement.

Theorem 2.23
.

Every matrix entry of
X
can be expressed as a Laurent polynomial in terms of cluster variables in the initial seed

(Q_{n},{\mathcal{F}}_{n},{\mathcal{P}}_{n})
of
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}_{n}={\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}(Q_{n},{\mathcal{F}}_{n},{\mathcal{P}}_{n})
as well as in seeds obtained from the initial one by a single mutation at any vertex other than vertices
\langle 2,2\rangle,\ldots,\langle n-1,n-1\rangle=(n-1,+)
.

Concequently, we get a Laurent representation for all matrix entries of
X
.
To finish the proof of completeness, we need to get another Laurent representation in some seed that does not contain

{\tt f}_{2n-2}(X)
as a cluster variable. We achieve this goal via a long sequence of mutations that starts with a mutation at
D
and results in a seed which evokes parallels to our construction in
[
19
]
and in which Laurentness can be established by combining properties of rational Poisson maps in
[
19
]
with
those of normal forms under the conjugation by triangular elements that we studied in
[
15
]
.

To this end, we define yet another family of
n(n-1)/2-1
matrices
{\tt K}_{ij}(X)
for
1\leq j<i\leq n
,

(i,j)\neq(n,1)
, of size
(n-j+2)\times(n-j+2)
whose entries are either zeros or entries of
X
, and put

{\tt k}_{ij}(X)=\det{\tt K}_{ij}(X)
. Additionally, we put
{\tt k}_{ii}(X)=\det X_{[i,n]}^{[i,n]}
for
i\in[1,n]
, so that

{\tt k}_{11}(X)=\det X
.

Theorem 2.24
.

(i)
There exists a sequence of mutations
\mathbf{W}_{n}
that starts with the initial seed in
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
and results in a seed with the set of mutable cluster variables given by

\{\phi_{kl}(X):k,l\geq 1,\ k+l\leq n-1\}\cup\{{\tt k}_{ij}(X):1\leq j\leq i\leq n,(i,j)\neq(1,1),(n,1)\}.

(ii)
All matrix entries of
X
are Laurent polynomials in terms of this seed that do not contain any of the frozen variables in the denominator.

Theorem
2.24
allows us to finish the proof of regularity and completeness for
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
.

6. To extend the presented construction of a generalized cluster structure to
\operatorname{Mat}_{n}
as mentioned in Remark
2.3
, we use once again the regular pullback construction described in
[
20
]
. The resulting almost-cluster structure is defined by
the quiver
\widehat{Q}_{n}
obtained from
Q_{n}
by adding two opposite arrows: one from
(1,1)
to
B
, and the other from
B
to
(1,1)
, see Fig.
7
.
Mutations of
\widehat{Q}_{n}
are performed as explained
in
[
20
, Remark 4.6]
, namely, if usual mutation rules produce
\Delta\neq 0
arrows between
(1,1)
and
B
, then the quiver after the mutation has

\Delta
arrows in the corresponding direction and no arrows in the opposite direction, otherwise the quiver has a pair of opposite arrows between these vertices. For example, mutation at
(1,1)
retains the pair of the opposite arrows and adds arrows from
B
to
(2,1)
and from
(1,2)
to
B
. A subsequent mutation at
(2,1)
replaces the pair of opposite arrows by
n-1
arrows from
B
to
(1,1)
.
The functions attached to the vertices of
\widehat{Q}_{n}
are exactly the same as for
Q_{n}
. The only string
\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
is given by
\widehat{\mathcal{P}}=(1,{\tt c}_{1}(X),\dots,{\tt c}_{n-2}(X),1)
, so that (
2.22
) is replaced by

\phi_{11}(X)\hat{\phi}^{\prime}_{11}(X)\\
=\det Xx_{1n}\phi_{12}^{n-1}(X)+\sum_{r=1}^{n-2}{\tt c}_{r}(X)\phi_{21}^{r}(X)\phi_{12}^{n-1-r}(X)+\det Xx_{n1}\phi_{21}^{n-1}(X).

Figure 7.
Quiver
\widehat{Q}_{n}

Theorem 2.25
.

The almost-cluster structure
\widehat{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n}=\widehat{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}({\mathcal{F}}_{n},\widehat{Q}_{n},\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{n})
is a regular complete almost-cluster structure on
Mat_{n}
compatible with
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
.

3.
Map
\Psi
and the proof of Theorem
2.10

3.1.
Map
\Psi^{\prime}
and the proof of Theorem
2.10
(i)

As it was explained in detail in
[
18
]
, given a pair of BD-triples, pairs
(\Gamma_{1}^{\rm r},\Gamma_{1}^{\tt c})
and
(\Gamma_{2}^{\rm r},\Gamma_{2}^{\tt c})
define a partition of a matrix representing an element of
GL_{n}
into blocks called
X
-blocks and
Y
-blocks,
respectively, and maps
{\gamma^{\rm r}}
and
{\gamma^{\tt c}}
define gluing of the blocks into compound matrices having a staircase shape. When the pair of
triples is aperiodic, the corresponding compound matrices are finite, and their trailing minors form the initial cluster for the cluster structure compatible with the Poisson bracket defined by the pair of triples. If the aperiodicity condition is violated, the corresponding compound matrices are infinite, and arising cluster structures are generalized. An approach to the treatment
of this case is suggested in
[
17
]
based on utilizing a periodic staircase structure of the obtained infinite compound matrices. In
[
17
, Section 6]
we studied the simplest example of such a generalized cluster structure for
GL_{6}
. While the validity of this approach for the general case remains conjectural, it provides a necessary intuition.

In our case, there is only one
X
-block and only one
Y
-block, and they both coincide with the initial matrix. The
blocks are glued into an infinite compound matrix as shown in Fig.
8
. The periodic staircase structure is indicated in the same figure by dashed lines. The blocks of the periodic staircase structure are
(n+1)\times(n+1)
matrices
{\tt A}={\tt A}(X)
and
{\tt B}={\tt B}(X)
so that
{\tt A}_{[1,n]}^{[3,n+1]}=X^{[1,n-1]}
,
{\tt B}_{[1,n]}^{[1,1]}=X^{[n,n]}
,
and
{\tt B}_{[2,n+1]}^{[2,n+1]}=X
; all other entries of
{\tt A}
and
{\tt B}
are zeros.

Figure 8.
Periodic staircase structure for the infinite compound matrix

By
[
17
, Theorem 3.2]
, we expect that the coefficients of the generalized exchange relation are
the coefficients of the characteristic polynomial
\det(\lambda{\tt A}+{\tt B})
. Assuming
x_{1n}x_{n1}\neq 0
, one can use elementary row operations to rewrire it as

(3.1)

\displaystyle\det(\lambda{\tt A}+{\tt B})

\displaystyle=\det\left(\lambda\begin{bmatrix}0&0&x_{11}&\dots&x_{1,n-1}\\
0&0&&&\\
\vdots&\vdots&&X_{L}&\\
0&0&&&\\
0&0&0&\dots&0\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}x_{1n}&0&0&\dots&0\\
0&0&&&\\
\vdots&\vdots&&X_{R}&\\
0&0&&&\\
0&x_{n1}&x_{n2}&\dots&x_{nn}\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=x_{1n}\det X\det(\lambda U+\mathbf{1}_{n-1}),

where
X_{L}
and
X_{R}
are the Schur complements of
x_{n1}
and
x_{1n}
, respectively, that is,

(n-1)\times(n-1)
matrices given by

\displaystyle X_{L}

\displaystyle=X_{[2,n]}^{[1,n-1]}-X_{[2,n]}^{[n,n]}x_{1n}^{-1}X_{[1,1]}^{[1,n-1]},

\displaystyle X_{R}

\displaystyle=X_{[1,n-1]}^{[2,n]}-X_{[1,n-1]}^{[1,1]}x_{n1}^{-1}X_{[n,n]}^{[2,n]},

and
U=X_{L}X_{R}^{-1}
. Note that

(3.2)

\det U=\frac{x_{n1}}{x_{1n}},

so that
U\in GL_{n-1}
. This suggests that a map
\Psi^{\prime}:GL_{n}\to GL_{n-1}
defined
via
\Psi^{\prime}:X\mapsto U
can be utilized in constructing a generalized cluster structure on
GL_{n}
compatible with

{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
.

We start the proof of Theorem
2.10
(i) with a number of preliminary technical computations. First, we rewrite the definitions of
matrices
X_{L}
and
X_{R}
as

(3.3)

\displaystyle X_{L}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathbf{1}_{n-1}\end{bmatrix}(X-XE_{L}X)\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{1}_{n-1}\cr 0\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle X_{R}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{1}_{n-1}&0\end{bmatrix}(X-XE_{R}X)\begin{bmatrix}0\cr\mathbf{1}_{n-1}\end{bmatrix},

where
E_{L}=e_{n}x_{1n}^{-1}e_{1}^{T}
,
E_{R}=e_{1}x_{n1}^{-1}e_{n}^{T}
,

e_{1}
and
e_{n}
are the first and the last columns of
\mathbf{1}_{n}
.
By (
3.3
) we get

\displaystyle\delta X_{L}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathbf{1}_{n-1}\end{bmatrix}(\mathbf{1}_{n}-XE_{L})\delta X(\mathbf{1}_{n}-E_{L}X)\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{1}_{n-1}\cr 0\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\delta X_{R}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{1}_{n-1}&0\end{bmatrix}(\mathbf{1}_{n}-XE_{R})\delta X(\mathbf{1}_{n}-E_{R}X)\begin{bmatrix}0\cr\mathbf{1}_{n-1}\end{bmatrix},

so that

\displaystyle\delta U=\delta X_{L}X_{R}^{-1}-U\delta X_{R}X_{R}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathbf{1}_{n-1}\end{bmatrix}(\mathbf{1}_{n}-XE_{L})\delta X(\mathbf{1}_{n}-E_{L}X)\begin{bmatrix}X_{R}^{-1}\cr 0\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle-\begin{bmatrix}U&0\end{bmatrix}(\mathbf{1}_{n}-XE_{R})\delta X(\mathbf{1}_{n}-E_{R}X)\begin{bmatrix}0\cr X_{R}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}.

It follows that the gradients
\nabla(f\circ\Psi^{\prime})
at a point
X\in GL_{n}
and

{\nabla}f
at the point
U=\Psi^{\prime}(X)\in GL_{n-1}
are related via

\nabla(f\circ\Psi^{\prime})=(\mathbf{1}_{n}-E_{L}X)\begin{bmatrix}X_{R}^{-1}\cr 0\end{bmatrix}{\nabla}f\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathbf{1}_{n-1}\end{bmatrix}(\mathbf{1}_{n}-XE_{L})\\
-(\mathbf{1}_{n}-E_{R}X)\begin{bmatrix}0\cr X_{R}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}{\nabla}f\begin{bmatrix}U&0\end{bmatrix}(\mathbf{1}_{n}-XE_{R})\\
=(\mathbf{1}_{n}-E_{L}X)\begin{bmatrix}0&X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}f\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}(\mathbf{1}_{n}-XE_{L})-(\mathbf{1}_{n}-E_{R}X)\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fU&0\end{bmatrix}(\mathbf{1}_{n}-XE_{R}).

Note that (
3.3
) implies

(3.4)

\displaystyle X(\mathbf{1}_{n}-E_{L}X)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{n}-XE_{L})X=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr X_{L}&0\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle X(\mathbf{1}_{n}-E_{R}X)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{n}-XE_{R})X=\begin{bmatrix}0&X_{R}\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix},

so that the left and right gradients of
f\circ\Psi^{\prime}
can be written as

\displaystyle\nabla(f\circ\Psi^{\prime})X

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{n}-E_{L}X)\begin{bmatrix}X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L}&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}-(\mathbf{1}_{n}-E_{R}X)\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle X\nabla(f\circ\Psi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&\cr 0&U{\nabla}f\end{bmatrix}(\mathbf{1}_{n}-XE_{L})-\begin{bmatrix}{\nabla}fU&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}(\mathbf{1}_{n}-XE_{R}).

Introducing

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle=-x_{1n}^{-1}X_{[1,1]}^{[1,n-1]}X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L},\qquad

\displaystyle\zeta=-x_{n1}^{-1}X_{[n,n]}^{[2,n]}X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L},

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle=-U{\nabla}fX_{[2,n]}^{[n,n]}x_{1n}^{-1},\qquad

\displaystyle\nu=-{\nabla}fUX^{[1,1]}_{[1,n-1]}x_{n1}^{-1},

we rewrite the expressions for the left and right gradients of
f\circ\Psi^{\prime}
as

(3.5)

\displaystyle\nabla(f\circ\Psi^{\prime})X

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L}&0\cr\eta&0\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}0&\zeta\cr 0&X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle X\nabla(f\circ\Psi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&\cr\xi&U{\nabla}f\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}{\nabla}fU&\nu\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}.

We now move on to the computation of the bracket
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
. It is convenient to re-write
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
in a way where the Lie-theoretic definition of
\gamma
is replaced by a more concrete transformation on the standard matrix
representation of

\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{n}
. Let
E_{kj}
denote the
n\times n
matrix having one at position
(k,j)
and zeros at all other positions. Put
\bar{S}=E_{12}+\dots+E_{n-1,n}
and
S=\bar{S}^{T}

and define the action of
\gamma
and
\gamma^{*}
on
\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{n}
via

\gamma(A)=SA\bar{S},\qquad\gamma^{*}(A)=\bar{S}AS.

Clearly, for
n\times n
matrices with an
(n-1)\times(n-1)
block
M
one has

\gamma\begin{bmatrix}M&\star\cr\star&\star\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&M\end{bmatrix},\qquad\gamma^{*}\begin{bmatrix}\star&\star\cr\star&M\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}M&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix},

where the exact values of
\star
are irrelevant.

By (
2.4
),
\bar{R}_{+}
on
\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{n}
can be written as

\bar{R}_{+}=\bar{R}_{0}+\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\pi_{>}-\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}\pi_{<}

with
\bar{R}_{0}
given by

(3.6)

\bar{R}_{0}(\eta)=\frac{n-1}{2n}\operatorname{Tr}\eta{\cdot}\mathbf{1}_{n}+\frac{1}{n}\left(\operatorname{Tr}\eta{\cdot}\bar{D}_{n}-\operatorname{Tr}(\bar{D}_{n}\eta){\cdot}\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)-\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}\eta,

where
\bar{D}_{n}=\operatorname{diag}(1,2,\dots,n)
, see
[
14
, Lemma 4.1]
.

Consequently, the components of
\bar{R}_{+}(\nabla(f\circ\Psi^{\prime})X)
are

\displaystyle\bar{R}_{0}\left(\begin{bmatrix}X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L}&0\cr\eta&0\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}0&\zeta\cr 0&X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L}\end{bmatrix}\right)=-\bar{R}_{0}(1-\gamma^{*})\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&(X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L})_{0}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L}&0\cr\eta&0\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}0&\zeta\cr 0&X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L}\end{bmatrix}\right)_{>}=-\begin{bmatrix}0&\zeta\cr 0&(X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L})_{>}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}\left(\begin{bmatrix}X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L}&0\cr\eta&0\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}0&\zeta\cr 0&X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L}\end{bmatrix}\right)_{<}=-\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&(X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L})_{<}\end{bmatrix};

here and in what follows
M_{>}=\pi_{>}(M)
,
M_{0}=\pi_{0}(M)
, and
M_{<}=\pi_{<}(M)
.

It follows from (
3.6
) that for a diagonal

(n-1)\times(n-1)
matrix
D
one has

\bar{R}_{0}(1-\gamma^{*})\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&D\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&D\end{bmatrix}-\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}D{\cdot}\mathbf{1}_{n}.

Summing up the components of
\bar{R}_{+}(\nabla(f\circ\Psi^{\prime})X)
we arrive at

(3.7)

\bar{R}_{+}(\nabla(f\circ\Psi^{\prime})X)=-\begin{bmatrix}0&\zeta\cr 0&X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L}\end{bmatrix}+\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}(X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L}){\cdot}\mathbf{1}_{n}\\
=-(\mathbf{1}_{n}-E_{R}X)\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L}\end{bmatrix}+\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}(X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L}){\cdot}\mathbf{1}_{n}.

Therefore, the first term in the right hand side of (
2.3
) is

\left\langle\bar{R}_{+}(\nabla(f_{1}\circ\Psi^{\prime})X),\nabla(f_{2}\circ\Psi^{\prime})X\right\rangle\\
=-\left\langle(\mathbf{1}_{n}-E_{R}X)\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}f_{1}X_{L}\end{bmatrix},(\mathbf{1}_{n}-E_{L}X)\begin{bmatrix}X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}f_{2}X_{L}&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle\\
+\left\langle\begin{bmatrix}0&\zeta(f_{1})\cr 0&X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}f_{1}X_{L}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}0&\zeta(f_{2})\cr 0&X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}f_{2}X_{L}\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle+\left\langle\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}(X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}fX_{L}){\cdot}\mathbf{1}_{n},\nabla(f_{2}\circ\Psi^{\prime})X\right\rangle.

It follows immediately from (
3.5
) that the left gradient belongs to
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
, and hence the third term
in the right hand above vanishes.
Further, the second term is just
\langle X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}f_{1}X_{L},X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}f_{2}X_{L}\rangle
.
Using (
3.4
) we rewrite the first term as

-\left\langle X^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}0&X_{R}\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}f_{1}X_{L}\end{bmatrix},X^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr X_{L}&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}f_{2}X_{L}&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle,

so that finally

(3.8)

\left\langle\bar{R}_{+}(\nabla(f_{1}\circ\Psi^{\prime})X),\nabla(f_{2}\circ\Psi^{\prime})X\right\rangle\\
=-\left\langle X^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}0&{\nabla}f_{1}X_{L}\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix},X^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr U{\nabla}f_{2}X_{L}&0\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle+\langle X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}f_{1}X_{L},X_{R}^{-1}{\nabla}f_{2}X_{L}\rangle.

We proceed by computing
R_{+}(X\nabla(f\circ\Psi^{\prime}))
with
R_{+}
given on
\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{n}
by

R_{+}=R_{0}+\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\pi_{>}-\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\pi_{<}

and
R_{0}
defined via

(3.9)

R_{0}(\eta)=\frac{n-1}{2n}\operatorname{Tr}\eta{\cdot}\mathbf{1}_{n}+\frac{1}{n}\left(\operatorname{Tr}\eta{\cdot}D_{n}-\operatorname{Tr}(D_{n}\eta){\cdot}\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)-\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\eta

with
D_{n}=\operatorname{diag}(n,\dots,2,1)
similarly to
\bar{R}_{0}
above.

The components of
R_{+}(X\nabla(f\circ\Psi^{\prime}))
are

\displaystyle R_{0}\left(\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr\xi&U{\nabla}f\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}{\nabla}fU&\nu\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}\right)=R_{0}\left(\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&(U{\nabla}f)_{0}\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}({\nabla}fU)_{0}&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}\right),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\left(\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr\xi&U{\nabla}f\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}{\nabla}fU&\nu\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}\right)_{>}=-\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&[{\nabla}f,U]_{>}\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}({\nabla}fU)_{>}&\nu\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr\xi&U{\nabla}f\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}{\nabla}fU&\nu\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}\right)_{<}=-\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\begin{bmatrix}[{\nabla}f,U]_{<}&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}({\nabla}fU)_{<}&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}.

Note that

\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr\xi&U{\nabla}f\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}{\nabla}fU&\nu\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}=-\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr\xi&[{\nabla}f,U]\end{bmatrix}-(1-\gamma)\begin{bmatrix}{\nabla}fU&\nu\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix},

and both matrices on the right hand side belong to
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
.
It follows from (
3.9
) that for a diagonal
(n-1)\times(n-1)
matrix
D
one has

R_{0}(1-\gamma)\begin{bmatrix}D&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}D&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}-\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}D{\cdot}\mathbf{1}_{n},

and hence

R_{0}\left(\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&(U{\nabla}f)_{0}\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}({\nabla}fU)_{0}&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}\right)\\
=-R_{0}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&[{\nabla}f,U]_{0}\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}({\nabla}fU)_{0}&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}+\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}(\nabla fU){\cdot}\mathbf{1}_{n}.

Summing up the components of
R_{+}(X\nabla(f\circ\Psi^{\prime}))
we arrive at

(3.10)

R_{+}(X\nabla(f\circ\Psi^{\prime}))=-R_{0}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&[{\nabla}f,U]_{0}\end{bmatrix}+\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}(\nabla fU){\cdot}\mathbf{1}_{n}\\
-\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&[{\nabla}f,U]_{>}\end{bmatrix}+\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&[{\nabla}f,U]_{<}\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}{\nabla}fU&\nu\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}.

To compute the second term in the right hand side of (
2.3
) we treat separately the contributions of
the terms in (
3.10
) to
\left\langle R_{+}(X\nabla(f_{1}\circ\Psi^{\prime})),X\nabla(f_{2}\circ\Psi^{\prime})\right\rangle
.
The contribution of the first term is the sum of

\left\langle-R_{0}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&[{\nabla}f_{1},U]_{0}\end{bmatrix},-\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&[{\nabla}f_{2},U]_{0}\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle=\left\langle R_{0}[{\nabla}f_{1},U]_{0},[{\nabla}f_{2},U]_{0}\right\rangle

(note that
R_{0}
in the right hand side above is the operator on
\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{n-1}
associated with the corresponding Cremmer–Gervais data)
and

\left\langle-R_{0}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&[{\nabla}f_{1},U]_{0}\end{bmatrix},-(1-\gamma)\begin{bmatrix}({\nabla}f_{2}U)_{0}&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle\\
=\left\langle\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&[{\nabla}f_{1},U]_{0}\end{bmatrix},(1-\gamma)\gamma^{*}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&({\nabla}f_{2}U)_{0}\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle\\
=\left\langle\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&[{\nabla}f_{1},U]_{0}\end{bmatrix},\frac{(1-\gamma)\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&({\nabla}f_{2}U)_{0}\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle\\
=-\left\langle[{\nabla}f_{1},U]_{0},({\nabla}f_{2}U)_{0}\right\rangle;

here the last equality follows from the fact that

\gamma\gamma^{*}
acts by multiplication by

\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\cr 0&\mathbf{1}_{n-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
on the left.
To justify the first equality note that by
[
18
, Lemma 4.2]
,
R_{0}
on the Cartan subalgebra of
sl_{n}
is given by

R_{0}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{1-\gamma}-\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}\right)
; further, one can readily check that on this subalgebra

\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}+\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{*}}=0
, so that finally
R_{0}=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}
. The contribution
of the second term vanishes since by (
3.5
) the right gradient belongs to
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
.

The contribution of the third and the fourth terms is the sum of

\left\langle-\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&[{\nabla}f_{1},U]_{>}\end{bmatrix}+\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&[{\nabla}f_{1},U]_{<}\end{bmatrix},-\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&[{\nabla}f_{2},U]\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle\\
=\left\langle\frac{1}{1-\gamma}[{\nabla}f_{1},U]_{>}-\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}[{\nabla}f_{1},U]_{<},[{\nabla}f_{2},U]\right\rangle

and

\left\langle-\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&[{\nabla}f_{1},U]_{>}\end{bmatrix}+\frac{\gamma^{*}}{1-\gamma^{*}}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\cr 0&[{\nabla}f_{1},U]_{<}\end{bmatrix},-(1-\gamma)\begin{bmatrix}{\nabla}f_{2}U&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle\\
=\left\langle\frac{(1-\gamma^{*})\gamma}{1-\gamma}\begin{bmatrix}[{\nabla}f_{1},U]_{>}&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}[{\nabla}f_{1},U]_{<}&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}{\nabla}f_{2}U&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle\\
=\left\langle-\begin{bmatrix}[{\nabla}f_{1},U]&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}[{\nabla}f_{1},U]_{0}&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}{\nabla}f_{2}U&0\cr 0&0\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle\\
=-\left\langle[{\nabla}f_{1},U],{\nabla}f_{2}U\right\rangle+\left\langle[{\nabla}f_{1},U]_{0},({\nabla}f_{2}U)_{0}\right\rangle;

here the second equality follows from the fact that

\gamma^{*}\gamma
acts by multiplication by
