The solution of (
7.9
) is given by

{\scriptstyle\Upsilon}_{j}=x_{n,j+1},\quad j\in[1,n-2],\qquad\Upsilon=\begin{bmatrix}x_{n1}&0&\dots&0\\
x_{n2}&x_{n1}&\dots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&0\\
x_{n,n-2}&\dots&x_{n2}&x_{n1}\end{bmatrix}C_{[1,n-2]}.

Proof.

Re-writing (
7.9
) row by row:

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{[1,1]}

\displaystyle=x_{n1}\mu,

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{[i+1,i+1]}

\displaystyle=\Upsilon_{[i,i]}M+{\scriptstyle\Upsilon}_{i}\mu,\quad i\in[1,n-3],

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle=\Upsilon_{[n-2,n-2]}M+{\scriptstyle\Upsilon}_{n-2}\mu,

one sees that (
7.9
) is equivalent to

(7.10)

\begin{bmatrix}\Upsilon\\
m\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}x_{n1}&0&\dots&0\\
{\scriptstyle\Upsilon}_{1}&x_{n1}&\dots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&0\\
{\scriptstyle\Upsilon}_{n-2}&\dots&{\scriptstyle\Upsilon}_{1}&x_{n1}\end{bmatrix}C.

Comparing the last row above with Lemma
7.1
, we obtain the desired result for
{\scriptstyle\Upsilon}_{j}
, and the formula for
\Upsilon
follows.
∎

Now we can use (
7.8
),(
7.5
),(
7.3
),(
7.1
) to restore
X_{[2,n-1]}
from
U,X_{\{1,n\}}
. First, define

\displaystyle\Theta=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&V_{+}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}N^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle L=\begin{bmatrix}x_{n1}&0&\dots&0\\
x_{n2}&x_{n1}&\dots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&0\\
x_{n,n-1}&\dots&x_{n2}&x_{n1}\end{bmatrix}C.

Proposition 7.3
.

(7.11)

X=\Theta\cdot\left(\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
L&0\end{bmatrix}+X_{[n,n]}^{T}x_{1n}^{-1}X_{[1,1]}\right).

Proof.

From (
7.8
) and Lemma
7.2
,

\displaystyle\tilde{y}

\displaystyle=\left(N_{[2,n-1]}^{[2,n-1]}\right)^{-1}(X_{[n,n]}^{[2,n-1]})^{T}-\left(N_{[2,n-1]}^{[2,n-1]}\right)^{-1}N^{[1,1]}_{[2,n-1]}x_{1n}

\displaystyle=\left(N_{[2,n-1]}^{[2,n-1]}\right)^{-1}(X_{[n,n]}^{[2,n-1]})^{T}-e_{1}^{T}\frac{x_{n1}}{n_{11}}

\displaystyle=\left(N^{-1}\right)_{[2,n-1]}(X_{[n,n]}^{[1,n-1]})^{T},

thus

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=\tilde{y}-x_{nn}v_{[1,n-2]}=\left[\left(N^{-1}\right)_{[2,n-1]}\ (-v_{[1,n-2]})\right]X_{[n,n]}^{T},

\displaystyle X^{[n,n]}_{[2,n-1]}

\displaystyle=(V_{+})_{[1,n-2]}^{[1,n-2]}\left[\left(N^{-1}\right)_{[2,n-1]}\ (-v_{[1,n-2]})\right]X_{[n,n]}^{T}.

Note that

-(V_{+})_{[1,n-2]}^{[1,n-2]}v_{[1,n-2]}=(V_{+})_{[1,n-2]}^{[n-1,n-1]},

so that we get

X^{[n,n]}_{[2,n-1]}=(V_{+})_{[1,n-2]}\begin{bmatrix}\left(N^{-1}\right)_{[2,n-1]}&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}X_{[n,n]}^{T}=\Theta_{[2,n-1]}X_{[n,n]}^{T}.

Similarly,

{\widetilde{Y}}=\left(N_{[2,n-1]}^{[2,n-1]}\right)^{-1}\Upsilon,\qquad Y=\left[\left(N_{[2,n-1]}^{[2,n-1]}\right)^{-1}\ (-v_{[1,n-2]})\right]\begin{bmatrix}\Upsilon\\
m\end{bmatrix},

and

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{[2,n-1]}^{[1,n-1]}

\displaystyle=(V_{+})_{[1,n-2]}^{[1,n-2]}Y=(V_{+})_{[1,n-2]}^{[1,n-2]}\left[\left(N_{[2,n-1]}^{[2,n-1]}\right)^{-1}\ (-v_{[1,n-2]})\right]\begin{bmatrix}\Upsilon\\
m\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{[2,n-1]}^{[2,n]}L.

Finally,

\displaystyle X_{[2,n-1]}^{[1,n-1]}

\displaystyle=\tilde{X}_{[2,n-1]}^{[1,n-1]}+X^{[n,n]}_{[2,n-1]}x_{1n}^{-1}X_{[1,1]}^{[1,n-1]}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{[2,n-1]}\left(\begin{bmatrix}0\\
L\end{bmatrix}+X_{[n,n]}^{T}x_{1n}^{-1}X_{[1,1]}^{[1,n-1]}\right).

Taking into account that
m=L_{[n-1,n-1]}
by Lemma
7.1
and using (
7.7
) we obtain the desired formula for
X
.
∎

To finish the proof of Theorem
2.23
we need the following auxiliary statement.
Let
\overline{\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}}({{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n-1}^{\dagger})
be the generalized upper cluster algebra defined by the generalized cluster structure
{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n-1}^{\dagger}
and
\overline{\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}}(\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}^{T})
be the upper cluster algebra defined by the cluster structure

\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}^{T}
, where in both cases the ground ring is the corresponding polynomial ring in frozen variables.
By Theorem
2.5
and Remark
2.6
,
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}({{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n-1}^{\dagger})
is obtained from

\overline{\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}}({{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n-1}^{\dagger})
by localizing at
\det U
. Similarly, by Theorem
2.8
and Remark
2.9
,
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}(\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}^{T})
is obtained from
\overline{\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}}(\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}^{T})
by localizing at
t_{n-1}^{+}
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
\psi^{\prime}
be an element of
\overline{\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}}({{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n-1}^{\dagger})
and
\psi^{\prime\prime}
be an element of
\overline{\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}}(\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}^{T})
. Then
\psi^{\prime}(\Psi^{\prime}(X))
and
\psi^{\prime\prime}(\Psi^{\prime\prime}(X))
are elements of the generalized upper cluster algebra
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}({{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n})
divided by monomials in
x_{1n}
,
x_{n1}
, and
\det X
.

Proof.

We prove the claim for
\psi^{\prime}
, a similar argument applies to
\psi^{\prime\prime}
as well. In view of the general construction described in Section
2.7
and of Proposition
2.14
, standard quasi-isomorphism considerations imply that any cluster variable in
{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n-1}^{\dagger}

evaluated at the image of
\Psi^{\prime}(X)
is equal to the corresponding cluster variable in
{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n}
times a Laurent monomial in frozen variables
x_{1n}
,
x_{n1}
,
\det X
. Since
\psi^{\prime}\in\overline{\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}}({{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n-1}^{\dagger})
, it has a Laurent polynomial expression in all seeds adjacent to
({\mathcal{D}}_{n-1},Q_{n-1}^{\dagger},{\mathcal{P}}_{n-1})
. Therefore,
\psi^{\prime}(\Psi^{\prime}(X))
has a Laurent polynomial expression in all seeds adjacent to the initial seed in
{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n}
in all directions
(k,l)
and
\langle k,l\rangle
. Direction
\langle n-1,n-1\rangle
needs to be considered separately since the corresponding vertex is frozen in

{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n-1}^{\dagger}
, but the variable
{\tt g}_{n-1,n-1}(X)
enters
\psi^{\prime}(\Psi^{\prime}(X))
polynomially, hence the claim is still true. Finally, mutations in directions
(i,-)
,
i\in[1,n-1]
,
(i,+)
,
i\in[1,n-2]
, do not affect the Laurent polynomial expression of
\psi^{\prime}(\Psi^{\prime}(X))
. Thus
\psi^{\prime}(\Psi^{\prime}(X))
has Laurent polynomial expression in the star of the initial seed in
{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n}
with denominators possibly containing
x_{1n}
,
x_{n1}
,
\det X
, and the assertion of the lemma follows.
∎

We are now ready to complete the proof of Theorem
2.23
. By Proposition
7.3
,
X
can be expressed as a product of two matrices. The entries of the second matrix are, up to a Laurent monomial in
x_{1n}

and
x_{n1}
, polynomials in the entries of the first and the last row of
X
. Then the definition of the map
\Psi^{\prime\prime}
implies that each of this entries is a polynomial
P
in coefficients of
\bar{q}(\lambda)
,
p(\lambda)
defined in (
3.12
) times a Laurent monomial in
x_{1n}
and
x_{n1}
. As explained above,
P
belongs to the upper cluster algebra
\overline{\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}}(\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}^{T})
localized at
t^{+}_{n-1}
. Using Lemma
7.4

and (
2.20
), we conclude that every matrix entry of the second factor in (
7.11
) is an element of

\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}({{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n})
divided by a monomial in
{\tt f}_{2n-2}={\tt g}_{n-1,n-1}
and
x_{1n},x_{n1}
.

On the other hand, the entries of
\Theta
in (
7.11
) are rational functions in matrix entries of

U=\Psi^{\prime}(X)
. It follows from the definitions of
V
,
N
, and
\Theta
that denominators of these functions written in terms of matrix entries of
V
are monomials in terms of trailing principal minors of
V
.
By Corollary
4.2
and (
4.6
),

\det V_{[j,n-1]}^{[j,n-1]}=\begin{cases}\dfrac{g_{jj}(U)}{g_{j-1,j-1}(U)}\quad&\text{if $j\geq 3$},\\
g_{jj}(U)\quad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Moreover, (
4.5
) together with (
4.6
) show that every matrix entry
v_{ij}
for
j>2
is a polynomial in
U
(and therefore an element of
\overline{\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}}({{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n-1}^{\dagger})
) divided by
g_{j-1,j-1}(U)
, while

v_{i1}=u_{i1}
,
v_{i2}=u_{i2}
,
i\in[1,n-1]
.
Using once again Lemma
7.4
together with (
2.18
), we see that every matrix entry of
\Theta
is an element of
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}({\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}_{n})
divided by a monomial that may contain only
{\tt g}_{22},\ldots,{\tt g}_{n-1,n-1}
and frozen variables
x_{1n},x_{n1},\det X
. Consequently, the same is true for matrix entries of
X
. ∎

Remark 7.5
.

One can show that the matrix
L
featured in Proposition
7.3
is, in fact, symmetric with entries given by

L_{ij}=-\frac{1}{x_{1n}}\sum_{s=0}^{\min(i-1,n-j-1)}\det X_{1n}^{i-s,j+1+s}

for
i\leq j
. However, this explicit formula is not needed in the proof of Theorem
2.23
, and we omit its derivation.

7.2.
Mutation sequence
\mathbf{W}_{n}
: proof of Theorem
2.24
(i)

7.2.1.
Quiver
Q_{n}^{0}
and universal notation

We start with defining matrices

{\tt K}_{ij}(X)
of size
(n-j+2)\times(n-j+2)
for
1\leq j<i\leq n
,
(i,j)\neq(n,1)
. These are
maximal trailing submatrices
of matrices of type
{\tt G}(I_{1},I_{2})
, that is, square submatrices that occupy all rows and the last

|I_{1}|+|I_{2}|
columns (see Section
4.2
immediately after the proof of Lemma
4.6
for the definition of such matrices). The subsets
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
that correspond to
{\tt K}_{ij}
are
I_{1}=[i,n]
and
I_{2}=[n+j-i,n]
. Recall that
{\tt k}_{ij}(X)=\det{\tt K}_{ij}(X)
for
1\leq j<i\leq n
,
(i,j)\neq(n,1)
, and
{\tt k}_{ii}(X)=\det X_{[i,n]}^{[i,n]}
.

Since functions
\phi_{kl}(X)
,
k,l\geq 1
,
k+l\leq n-1
, and
{\tt k}_{nn}(X)=f_{1}(X)
are already present in the initial cluster
{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
, we invoke
[
3
, Th. 3.6]
that states that if two seeds are mutationally equivalent and share a set of common cluster variables, there exists a sequence of mutations that connects these
seeds and does not involve the common cluster variables. Consequently, we will have to mutate the subquiver
Q_{n}^{0}
of the initial quiver
Q_{n}
that does not contain vertices
(k,l)
for
k+l<n-1
. The vertices
(k,n-k-1)
for
1\leq k\leq n-2
enter
Q_{n}^{0}
as frozen, same as vertices
B
and
C
; vertex
(1,-)
is frozen as well, so that vertex
A
is not contained in
Q_{n}^{0}
. For convenience of describing the mutation sequence, we introduce a universal numbering of the vertices of quivers
Q_{n}^{0}
, so that the same vertex gets the same number in all
Q_{n}^{0}
for different
n
. Vertex
D
gets number 0, mutable vertices in the part of
Q_{n}^{0}
that corresponds to
Q_{n-1}^{\dagger}
are numbered by positive integers from 1 to
n(n-3)/2
layer by layer starting from the layer closest to
D
, bottom up in each layer.
Frozen vertices in this part of
Q_{n}^{0}
are numbered by positive integers from
n(n-3)/2+1
to
n(n-1)/2
as follows: first
B
, then
C
, and then the vertices
(k,n-k-1)
bottom up. Mutable vertices in the part of
Q_{n}^{0}
that corresponds to
Q_{n}^{T}
are numbered by negative integers from
-1
to
-2n+4

layer by layer starting from the layer closest to
D
, bottom up in each layer. The remaining frozen vertex is numbered
-2n+3
. The quiver
Q_{6}^{0}
with the universal numbering of its vertices is shown in Fig.
9
.

Figure 9.
Quiver
Q_{6}^{0}

Our goal is to find a mutation sequence that takes cluster variables that correspond to mutable vertices of
Q_{n}^{0}
to
the functions
{\tt k}_{ij}(X)
for
1\leq j\leq i\leq n
,
(i,j)\neq(1,1),(n,1),(n,n)
.
We will see that all cluster variables that arise along the mutation path (excluding
{\tt k}_{ij}
themselves) are determinants of maximal trailing submatrices of certain matrices
{\tt G}(I_{1},\dots,I_{p})
of the folowing two types:

(i)
I_{j}
for
j\in[1,p]
consists of the first row and
|I_{j}|-1>0
last rows, or

(ii)
I_{1}
consists of the
|I_{1}|>0
last rows, and
I_{j}
for
j\in[2,p]
consists of the first row and
|I_{j}|-1>0
last rows.

To facilitate notation, the functions of the first type are denoted
[k_{1}\,k_{2}\ldots k_{p}]
, and the functions of the second type,
[\bar{k}_{1}\,k_{2}\ldots k_{p}]
, where
k_{j}=|I_{j}|
. Note that the symbol
[k_{1}\,k_{2}\ldots k_{p}]
defines a function whenever
n\geq k_{i}
,
i\in[1,p]
, and
n+p-1\geq\sum_{i=1}^{p}k_{i}
.
If
r
consecutive values of
k_{j}
are equal to the same value
k
, we occasionally write
k^{r}
instead of

k\ldots k
. The corresponding matrices are denoted
{\tt G}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{p})
and
{\tt G}(\bar{k}_{1},\ldots,k_{p})
, respectively.
Adopting this notation, we write the cluster variable
{\tt g}_{n-1,n-1}(X)
at the vertex 0 of
Q_{n}^{0}
as
[2^{n-1}]
. Further, cluster variables at the vertices of the
k
th layer to the left of 0 bottom up are
[2^{n-k-3}\ 2\ k+2]
,
[2^{n-k-3}\ 3\ k+1],\ldots,[2^{n-k-3}\ k+2\ 2]
. The cluster variable at
B
is

[n]
, the cluster variables at the frozen layer bottom up are
[\bar{2}\ n-1]
,
[\bar{3}\ n-2],\ldots,[\overline{n-1}\ 2]
. The variable at vertex
-2k+1
is
[\bar{1}\ 2^{n-k-1}]
, the variable at vertex
-2k
is
[2^{n-k-1}]
.

We define the
promotion

of the functions described above as follows: the promotion of
[k_{1}\ldots k_{p}]
is
[2\,k_{1}\ldots k_{p}]
,
the promotion of
[\bar{1}\,k_{2}\ldots k_{p}]
is
[\bar{1}\,2\,k_{2}\ldots k_{p}]
,
the promotion of
[\bar{k}_{1}\,k_{2}\ldots k_{p}]
for
k_{1}>1
is
[\bar{1}\,k_{1}+1\,k_{2}\ldots k_{p}]
.
The
demotion
of a promoted function is defined as follows: the demotion of
[2\,k_{1}\ldots k_{p}]
is

[\bar{1}\,k_{1}\ldots k_{p}]
, and the demotion of
[\bar{1}\,k_{1}\ldots k_{p}]
is
[k_{1}\ldots k_{p}]
. Note that promotion and demotion are not inverse of each other; they commute if both are applicable.
Promotions and demotions of matrices
{\tt G}
are defined in a similar way.
Note that for any mutable vertex of
Q_{n-1}^{0}
with the universal number
i
, the variable attached to
i
in

Q_{n}^{0}
is the promotion of the variable attached to
i
in
Q_{n-1}^{0}
. We call this the
promotion property

for
Q_{n-1}^{0}
and
Q_{n}^{0}
. Assume that
Q_{n-1}^{\prime}
and
Q_{n}^{\prime}
are obtained from
Q_{n-1}^{0}
and
Q_{n}^{0}
, respectively, via the same sequence of mutations. We say that the promotion property is valid for
Q_{n-1}^{\prime}
and
Q_{n}^{\prime}
at
i
if the variable attached to
i
in
Q_{n}^{\prime}
is the promotion of the variable attached to
i
in
Q_{n-1}^{\prime}
.

Using notation introduced above, exchange relation (
2.21
) at vertex
D
reads

(7.12)

[2^{n-1}]\cdot[\bar{1}\ 2^{n-4}\ 3]=[\bar{1}\ 2^{n-2}]\cdot[2^{n-3}\ 3]+[2^{n-2}]\cdot[\bar{1}\ 2^{n-4}\ 3\ 2].

Note that the last term in the right hand side corresponds to the product of
x_{n1}
and
{\tt g}_{n-1,n-2}(X)
; in all mutations along the sequence we are looking for that involve vertex
C
,
x_{n1}
enters exchange relations in a similar way.
Recall that relation (
7.12
) was obtained in Section
5.3
as the four-term Plücker relation for the matrix obtained by prepending rows
[0,\dots,0,1]
and
[1,0,\dots,0]
to
{\tt G}(2^{n-3},3,2)
and choosing
the indices as follows:
\alpha
and
\beta
correspond to the prepended rows,
\gamma
to the first row of the first block row,
\delta
to the second row of the penultimate block row,
\xi
and
\zeta
to the rows of the last block row. Slightly abusing terminology, we call it the four-term Plücker relation for
{\tt G}(2^{n-3},3,2)
.
More generally, the four-term Plücker relation for
{\tt G}(k_{1},\dots,k_{p-1},2)
is obtained by prepending rows

[0,\dots,0,1]
and
[1,0,\dots,0]
to its last
\sum_{i=1}^{p}k_{i}-1
columns and defining
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
,

\delta
,
\xi
, and
\zeta
in the same way as above. Note that one of the terms in such relations vanishes identically, so, in fact, they have only three terms. In particular, the four-term Plücker relation for

{\tt G}(k,2)
with
k>2
reads

(7.13)

[k-1\,2]\cdot[\overline{k-1}]=[\overline{k-2}\,2]\cdot[k]+[\overline{k-1}\,2]\cdot[k-1].

The following statement is obtained straightforwardly from the definitions.

Proposition 7.6
.

(i)
The four-term Plücker relation for the promotion of a matrix
{\tt G}(k_{1},\dots,k_{p-1},2)
is obtained by promoting every function involved in the four-term Plücker relation for
{\tt G}(k_{1},\dots,k_{p-1},2)
.

(ii)
The four-term Plücker relation for the demotion of a promoted matrix is obtained by demoting every function involved in the four-term Plücker relation for the promoted matrix.

Consequently, the four-term Plücker relations for
{\tt G}(2^{q},k,2)
and its demotion
{\tt G}(\bar{1},2^{q-1},k,2)
read

(7.14)

\displaystyle[2^{q}\,k-1\,2]\cdot[\bar{1}\,2^{q-1}\,k]=[\bar{1}\,2^{q-1}\,k-1\,2]\cdot[2^{q}\,k]+[\bar{1}\,2^{q-1}\,k\,2]\cdot[2^{q}\,k-1],

\displaystyle[\bar{1}\,2^{q-1}\,k-1\,2]\cdot[2^{q-1}\,k]=[2^{q-1}\,k-1\,2]\cdot[\bar{1}\,2^{q-1}\,k]+[2^{q-1}\,k\,2]\cdot[\bar{1}\,2^{q-1}\,k-1].

We will also use the Desnanot–Jacobi identity for an
(r+1)\times r
matrix
A
and three rows
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
(see
[
14
, Eq. (6.2)]
) that reads

(7.15)

\widehat{\beta}\cdot\widehat{\alpha,\gamma}=\widehat{\gamma}\cdot\widehat{\alpha,\beta}+\widehat{\alpha}\cdot\widehat{\beta,\gamma},

where
\widehat{i}
denotes the
r\times r
minor of
A
obtained by deleting row
i
, and
\widehat{i,j}
denotes
the
(r-1)\times(r-1)
minor of
A
obtained by deleting rows
i
and
j
and column 1. The Desnanot–Jacobi identity
for
{\tt G}(k_{1},\dots,k_{p})
with
k_{p}>2
is obtained from the above relation when
A
is the submatrix in the last

\sum_{i=1}^{p}k_{i}-1
columns of
{\tt G}(k_{1},\dots,k_{p})
,

\alpha
is the first row of the first block row,
\beta
is the second row of the penultimate block row, and
\gamma
is the second row of the last block row of
{\tt G}(k_{1},\dots,k_{p})
. In particular, the Desnanot–Jacobi identity for
{\tt G}(k_{1},k_{2})
with
k_{1},k_{2}>2
reads

(7.16)

[k_{1}-1\,k_{2}]\cdot[\overline{k_{1}-1}\,k_{2}-1]=[k_{1}\,k_{2}-1]\cdot[\overline{k_{1}-2}\,k_{2}]+[\overline{k_{1}-1}\,k_{2}]\cdot[k_{1}-1\,k_{2}-1].

The following analog of Proposition
7.6
is obtained straightforwardly from the definitions.

Proposition 7.7
.

(i)
The Desnanot–Jacobi identity for the promotion of a matrix
{\tt G}(k_{1},\dots,k_{p})
is obtained by promoting every function involved in the Desnanot–Jacobi identity for
{\tt G}(k_{1},\dots,k_{p})
.

(ii)
The Desnanot–Jacobi identity for the demotion of a promoted matrix is obtained by demoting every function involved in the Desnanot–Jacobi identity for the promoted matrix.

Consequently, the Desnanot–Jacobi identities for
{\tt G}(2^{q},k_{1},k_{2})
with
k_{1},k_{2}>2
and its demotion

{\tt G}(\bar{1},2^{q-1},k_{1},k_{2})
read

(7.17)

\displaystyle\cdot[\bar{1}\,2^{q-1}\,k_{1}\,k_{2}-1]

\displaystyle=[2^{q}\,k_{1}\,k_{2}-1]\cdot[\bar{1}\,2^{q-1}\,k_{1}-1\,k_{2}]

\displaystyle+[\bar{1}\,2^{q-1}\,k_{1}\,k_{2}]\cdot[2^{q}\,k_{1}-1\,k_{2}-1],

\displaystyle[\bar{1}\,2^{q-1}\,k_{1}-1\,k_{2}]\cdot[2^{q-1}\,k_{1}\,k_{2}-1]

\displaystyle=[\bar{1}\,2^{q-1}\,k_{1}\,k_{2}-1]\cdot[2^{q-1}\,k_{1}-1\,k_{2}]

\displaystyle+[2^{q-1}\,k_{1}\,k_{2}]\cdot[\bar{1}\,2^{q-1}\,k_{1}-1\,k_{2}-1].

The sequence
\mathbf{W}_{n}
is defined as the concatenation of two sequences: the
head

\mathbf{H}_{n}
and the
tail

\mathbf{T}_{n}
, which will be treated separately.

7.2.2.
Sequence
\mathbf{H}_{n}

The sequence
\mathbf{H}_{n}
, in turn, is the concatenation of four subsequences: the
prefix

\mathbf{P}_{n}
, the
infix

\mathbf{I}_{n}
, the
root

\mathbf{R}_{n}
, and the
suffix

{\mathbf{S}}_{n}
. The root
\mathbf{R}_{n}
consists of the vertices in the
(n-3)
rd layer to the left of 0 bottom up. It is the leftmost mutable layer in

Q_{n}^{0}
, so that
\mathbf{R}_{4}=1\,2
,
\mathbf{R}_{5}=3\,4\,5
, etc.
The other three subsequences are defined recursively via

\mathbf{P}_{n}=\mathbf{H}_{n-1},\qquad\mathbf{I}_{n}=\mathbf{R}_{n-1}^{-}{\mathbf{S}}_{n-1}^{-},\qquad{\mathbf{S}}_{n}=\mathbf{I}_{n}^{-},

where
\mathbf{A}^{-}
means subtracting 1 from every index in
\mathbf{A}
. The recursion starts at
n=4
with
\mathbf{P}_{4}=0
,

\mathbf{I}_{4}=-1
, and
{\mathbf{S}}_{4}=-2
, so that
\mathbf{H}_{4}=0\,{-}1\,1\,2\,{-}2
. Further,
\mathbf{P}_{5}=0\,{-}1\,1\,2\,{-}2
,
\mathbf{I}_{5}=0\,1\,{-}3
,
{\mathbf{S}}_{5}={-}1\,0\,{-}4
, so that
\mathbf{H}_{5}=0\,{-}1\,1\,2\,{-}2\,0\,1\,{-}3\,3\,4\,5\,{-}1\,0\,{-}4
, etc.

Consider the quiver
Q_{n}^{n}
that has the same set of vertices as
Q_{n}^{0}
(both frozen and mutable) arranged in a different way. Vertices of
Q_{n}^{n}
are arranged into
n-1
boomerang shapes of sizes
n+1,n,\ldots,3
. Each boomerang shape contains a subset of vertices with consecutive numbers in descending order. The shapes are placed on the

(n+1)\times(n+1)
greed as shown in Fig.
10
for the case
n=6
. The vertex placed at the point with coordinates
i
and
j
is denoted
|i,j|
.

Figure 10.
Vertex arrangement for the quiver
Q_{6}^{6}

The arrows of
Q_{n}^{n}
are
|i,j|\to|i-1,j+1|
for
i\in[2,n+1]
,
j\in[1,n-i+2]
,
|i,j|\neq|2,2|
,
|i,j|\to|i,j-1|
for
i\in[2,n]
,
j\in[2,n-i+2]
,
|i,j|\neq|2,3|
,
|i,j|\to|i+1,j|
for
i\in[1,n-1]
,
j\in[2,n-i+1]
. Additionally, there is an oriented
path
|1,n+1|\to|3,1|\to|2,n|\to\cdots\to|n-k-1,k+3|\to|n-k+1,1|\to|n-k,k+2|\to\cdots\to|n-1,3|\to|n+1,1|\to|1,n+1|
.
The quiver
Q_{6}^{6}
is shown in Fig.
11
; the arrows in the additional path are shown by dashed lines.

Figure 11.
Quiver
Q_{6}^{6}

Lemma 7.8
.

Mutation sequence
\mathbf{H}_{n}
applied to the quiver
Q_{n}^{0}
results in
Q_{n}^{n}
. The variable attached to vertex
|i,j|
in
Q_{n}^{n}
is
[\overline{j-2}\ n-i-j+4]
for
j\geq 3
except for vertex
C=|1,3|
,
[n-i+2]
for

j=2
, and
[\overline{n-i+2}]
for
j=1
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
n
. For
n=4
, the exchange relations along the mutation path
\mathbf{H}_{4}
are

(7.18)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle:\quad[2^{3}]\cdot[\bar{1}\,3]=[\bar{1}\,2^{2}]\cdot[2\,3]+[2^{2}]\cdot[\bar{1}\,3\,2],

\displaystyle-1

\displaystyle:\quad[\bar{1}\,2^{2}]\cdot[3]=[\bar{1}\,3]\cdot[2^{2}]+[3\,2]\cdot[\bar{1}\,2],

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle:\quad[2\,3]\cdot[\bar{2}\,2]=[\bar{1}\,3]\cdot[3\,2]+[2^{2}]\cdot[\bar{2}\,3],

\displaystyle 2

\displaystyle:\quad[3\,2]\cdot[\bar{3}]=[\bar{2}\,2]\cdot[4]+[3]\cdot[\bar{3}\,2],

\displaystyle-2

\displaystyle:\quad[2^{2}]\cdot[\bar{2}]=[3]\cdot[\bar{1}\,2]+[\bar{2}\,2]\cdot[2].

A direct check shows that the assertions of the lemma hold true. Additionaly, one can check that for
n>4
the above mutation sequence adds the arrow from
3
to
C
and deletes the arrow from
5
to
3
.

Note that the first two exchange relations above are
particular cases of (
7.14
) for
k=3
and
q=2
,
the third relation is (
7.16
) for
k_{1}=k_{2}=3
, while the last two are (
7.13
) for
k=4
and
k=3
, respectively. We thus see that to handle the case
n=4
we have to consider matrices
{\tt G}(3,2)
(more exactly, its promotion
{\tt G}(2,3,2)
),
{\tt G}(3,3)
, and
{\tt G}(4,2)
.

Assume that the assertion of the lemma holds true for
\mathbf{H}_{n-1}
, and consider the evolution of
Q_{n}^{0}
and the associated cluster variables along the mutation sequence
\mathbf{H}_{n}
divided into seven consecutive segments:
\mathbf{H}_{n}=\mathbf{P}_{n-1}\mathbf{I}_{n-1}\mathbf{R}_{n-1}{\mathbf{S}}_{n-1}\mathbf{I}_{n}\mathbf{R}_{n}{\mathbf{S}}_{n}
. Recall that the promotion property for
Q_{n-1}^{0}
and
Q_{n}^{0}
is valid at every mutable vertex of
Q_{n-1}^{0}
. Moreover, mutations in the first two segments of
\mathbf{H}_{n}
are identical to mutations in the first two segments of
\mathbf{H}_{n-1}=\mathbf{P}_{n-1}\mathbf{I}_{n-1}\mathbf{R}_{n-1}{\mathbf{S}}_{n-1}
, that is, involve vertices with the same universal numbers connected with the same arrows. Therefore, by Propositions
7.6
and
7.7
,
the promotion property remains valid for all mutated vertices along the sequence.

Further, compare mutations in the third segment of
\mathbf{H}_{n-1}
and the third segment of
\mathbf{H}_{n}
. Recall that these
are mutations in the first from the left mutable layer of
Q_{n-1}^{0}
and the second from the left mutable layer of
Q_{n}^{0}
. The bottom vertex of this layer has universal number
i=(n-3)(n-4)/2
.
The arrows inside this layer and to the right of it are identical in both mutated quivers, as explained above.
An additional inductive assumption for the quiver obtained from
Q_{n}^{0}
claims the following:

(i) the arrow from the top vertex to
i
is deleted;

(ii) all other arrows inside this layer are the same as in
Q_{n}^{0}
;

(iii) the arrow from
i
to
i-1
is reversed;

(iv) there are arrows from
i
to
C
and from
C
to
i-1
.

The arrows on the left of this layer are nor involved in the previous mutations, so they remain exactly as in
Q_{n-1}^{0}
and
Q_{n}^{0}
, respectively. This assumption can be verified by direct observation for
n=5
.

We start the third segment with the mutation at
i
. The left neighborhood of
i
in the quiver obtained from
Q_{n-1}^{0}
before and after the mutation is shown in Fig.
12
a). The variable attached to the frozen vertex

j=(n-2)(n-3)/2+2
is
[\bar{2}\,n-2]
. A similar neighborhood in the quiver obtained from
Q_{n}^{0}
is shown in
Fig.
12
b). The variable attached to the mutable vertex
j-1
is
[3\,n-2]
. Additionally, in the second case, there is
x_{1n}
attached to vertex
C
. Note that the product of variables attached to
j-1
and
C
is
[\bar{1}\,3\,n-2]
, that is, the promotion of
[\bar{2}\,n-2]
. Consequently, Proposition
7.7
guarantees that the promotion property at
i
remains valid after this mutation. Moreover, the situation before the mutation at
i+1
is exactly the same as before, up to shifting all numbers up by one. Consequently, the promotion property at all mutable vertices of

Q_{n-1}^{0}
remains valid after the third segment is applied. Note that in the last mutation in this segment, the vertices that correspond to
i+1
and
j-1
in Fig.
12
b) are the bottom and the top vertices of the next layer, respectively, so that deletion of the arrow from
j-1
to
i+1
proves inductive assumption (i), reversal
of the arrows from
i+1
to
i
and from
i
to
C
and addition of the arrow from
i+1
to
C
proves inductive assumptions (iii) and (iv). All other mutations of this segment do not affect arrows in the leftmost mutable layer of

Q_{n}^{0}
, which proves inductive assumption (ii).

Figure 12.
Left neighborhood of
i
before and after the first mutation in
\mathbf{R}_{n-1}
: a) for
\mathbf{H}_{n-1}
;
b) for
\mathbf{H}_{n}

Finally, mutations in the fourth segment in
\mathbf{H}_{n-1}
and
\mathbf{H}_{n}
again involve only vertices with the same numbers connected with the same arrows, and hence by the end of the first four segments in
\mathbf{H}_{n}
the promotion property remains valid for all mutable vertices of
Q_{n-1}^{0}
. Taking into account the induction assumption concerning the
structure of
Q_{n-1}^{n-1}
, we can describe the quiver
Q_{n}^{n-1}
obtained from
Q_{n}^{0}
via the mutation sequence

\mathbf{H}_{n-1}
and the variables attached to its vertices, as compared to
Q_{n-1}^{n-1}
.

Quiver
Q_{n}^{n-1}
is placed on the grid, same as
Q_{n-1}^{n-1}
. The restriction of
Q_{n}^{n-1}
to positions
|i,j|
,

i\in[3,n+1]
,
j\in[1,n-i+2]
coincides with restriction of
Q_{n-1}^{n-1}
to positions
|i,j|
,
i\in[2,n]
,
j\in[1,n-i+1]
, so that the universal number for the vertex at position
|i,j|
in
Q_{n}^{n-1}
is the same as for
|i-1,j|
in
Q_{n-1}^{n-1}
. Consequently, by the promotion property, the variable attached to position
|i,j|
for
i\in[3,n+1]
,

j\in[1,n-i+2]
is
[\bar{1}\,j-1\,n-i-j+5]
for
j\geq 3
,
[2\,n-i+3]
for
j=2
, and
[\bar{1}\,n-i+2]

for
j=1
. The only difference between the two restrictions is that in
Q_{n-1}^{n-1}
vertices at positions
|2,2|
and
|n,1|
are frozen, while in
Q_{n}^{n-1}
the corresponding vertices at
|3,2|
and
|n+1,1|
are mutable. Frozen vertices from the leftmost layer of
Q_{n}^{0}
are placed at positions
|1,j|
for
j\in[3,n]
in the same vertical order. Vertices of the next layer are placed at positions
|2,j|
for
j\in[2,n]
in the same vertical order, except for the bottom mutable vertex
(n-2)(n-3)/2
that has been already placed at
|3,2|
. The arrows between the vertices in these two layers are not affected by the mutations in
\mathbf{H}_{n-1}
, except for the cases described in induction assumptions
(i)–(iv) that are already proved for this layer. Evolution of arrows between this layer and the layer to the right of it can be seen in Fig.
12
b). Finally, vertices
-2n+4
and
-2n+5
are placed at positions
|n+1,3|
and
|n+2,2|
, respectively. Arrows between them and the rest of the vertices are not affected by mutations in
\mathbf{H}_{n-1}
. The quiver
Q_{6}^{5}
is shown in Fig.
13
a). The part of
Q_{6}^{5}
that coincides with the described above part of
Q_{5}^{5}
is contained inside the triangle shown by a thin line.

Figure 13.
Quiver
Q_{6}^{5}
: a) the quiver; b) the part of
Q_{6}^{5}
relevant for the sequence
\mathbf{I}_{6}

By the promotion property, the variables inside this triangle are promotions of the
corresponding variables in
Q_{5}^{5}
, so that by the inductive assumption, the variable attached to
|i,j|
is

[\bar{1}\,j-1\,n-i-j+3]
. The variables outside the triangle retain their initial values. It is easy to see
that vertices that are mutated in the sequence
\mathbf{I}_{n}
fill the triangle
i\in[3,n-1]
,
j\in[3,n-i+2]
and that order is by columns from left to right, bottom up within each column. The corresponding vertices for the quiver
Q_{6}^{5}
are shown by double circles in Fig.
13
. Consequently, the relevant vertices outside the triangle
are
|2,j|
with
j\in[3,n-1]
, and the attached variables are
[j\,n-j+1]
. The part of
Q_{6}^{5}
relevant for the sequence
\mathbf{I}_{6}
is shown in Fig.
13
b).

Let us rearrange the vertices of
Q_{n}^{n-1}

after the sequence
\mathbf{I}_{n}
is applied in the following way: the vertices in rows
j=1,2
are shifted left by one, and the vertices
|i,j|
on the upper diagonal
i+j=n+2
are shifted down by
j
and right by one, so that they now form the lower row of the quiver. The result of this rearrangement for the quiver
Q_{6}^{5}
is shown in Fig.
14
.

Figure 14.
Quiver
Q_{6}^{5}
after mutation sequence
\mathbf{I}_{6}
and rearrangement

Comparing quivers
Q_{n}^{n-1}
(before the application of
\mathbf{I}_{n}
) and
Q_{n-1}^{n-2}
after the application of
\mathbf{I}_{n-1}
we observe that the subquiver of the latter quiver that is relevant for the sequence
\mathbf{R}_{n-1}{\mathbf{S}}_{n-1}
is isomorphic to
the subquiver of the former one that is relevant for the sequence
\mathbf{I}_{n}
. Moreover, the variable attached in the latter
quiver to the vertex that correspond to the vertex
|i,j|
of
Q_{n}^{n-1}
is
[j-1\,n-i-j+3]
. It is easy to see that
the variable
[\bar{1}\,j-1\,n-i-j+3]
attached to
|i,j|
in
Q_{n}^{n-1}
is obtained from
[j-1\,n-i-j+3]
by consecutive promotion and demotion, and the same holds true for all other vertices in the two isomorphic subquivers.
So, by the promotion and demotion properties, we conclude that all exchange relations along the sequence
\mathbf{I}_{n}
are
obtained by consecutive promotion and demotion of the exchange relations along the sequence
\mathbf{R}_{n-1}{\mathbf{S}}_{n-1}
. Therefore, the variable attached to the vertex
|i,j|
of the rearranged quiver is
[j-1\,n-i-j+4]
for
i\in[2,n-1]
,

j\in[3,n-i+2]
,
[\bar{1}\,n-i+2]
for
j=2
,
i\in[3,n]
,
[n-i+3]
for
i\in[3,n+1]
, and

[\overline{j-2}\,n-j+3]
for
i=1
,
j\in[4,n+1]
.

Further, the sequence
\mathbf{R}_{n}
is applied. It is easy to see that the vertices that participate in this sequence are the vertices in the second column of the current quiver bottom up (vertices
6,7,8,9
in Fig.
14
for
n=6
). The corresponding exchange relations are (
7.16
) for

k_{1}=j
and
k_{2}=n-j+2
with
j\in[3,n-1]
, and (
7.13
) for
k=n
. The resulting variables are
[\overline{j-1}\,n-j+1]
for
j\in[3,n-1]
and
[\overline{n-1}]
for
j=n
.

Finally, the sequence
{\mathbf{S}}_{n}
is applied. It is easy to see that the vertices that participate in this sequence fill the triangle
i\in[3,n-1]
,
j\in[3,n-i+2]
(it is shown by double circles in Fig.
14
). The order of mutations is
by columns from left to right, bottom up in each column. Direct inspection shows that the relevant part of the quiver is exactly the same as for sequences
\mathbf{R}_{n-1}{\mathbf{S}}_{n-1}
and
\mathbf{I}_{n}
, see Fig.
13
b) for the case
n=6
. Moreover,
the functions attached to the vertices involved in the mutations are exactly the same as in the case of
\mathbf{R}_{n-1}{\mathbf{S}}_{n-1}
, and hence the variables attached to the vertices after the mutation coincide with those obtained at the end of
\mathbf{H}_{n-1}
. It remains to rearrange the resulting quiver similarly to what was done before: the vertices in the rows
j=1,2

are shifted left by one, the vertices of the first column are shifted down by one, and the vertices
|i,j|
on the upper diagonal
i+j=n+2
are shifted down by
j
and right by one, so that they now form the lower row of the quiver. The result of this rearrangement coincides with the quiver
Q_{n}^{n}
.
∎

7.2.3.
Sequence
\mathbf{T}_{n}

Note that the variables attached to the first row of the quiver
Q_{n}^{n}
(see
Fig.
11
for
n=6
) are exactly

{\tt k}_{ii}(X)
, so we invoke once again
[
3
, Th. 3.6]
and freeze them.
The remaining mutable vertices are mutated by columns from right to left (in a contrast with mutations in
\mathbf{H}_{n}
). As we shall see, mutations in the same column commute.

The exchange relations
are Desnanot–Jacobi identities for matrices
{\tt G}(\bar{k}_{1},k_{2})
with
k_{2}>2
that are obtained from (
7.15
)
when
A
is the submatrix in the last
k_{1}+k_{2}-1
columns of
{\tt G}(\bar{k}_{1},k_{2})
,

\alpha
is the first row of the first block row,
\beta
is the first row of the second block row, and
\gamma
is the second row of the second block row of
{\tt G}(\bar{k}_{1},k_{2})
. Consequently, the corresponding Desnanot–Jacobi identity for
k_{1}>1
reads

(7.19)

[\overline{k_{1}-1}\,k_{2}-1]\cdot[\bar{k}_{1}\,\overline{k_{2}-1}]=[\bar{k}_{1}\,k_{2}-1]\cdot[\overline{k_{1}-1}\,\overline{k_{2}-1}]+[\overline{k_{1}-1}\,k_{2}]\cdot[\bar{k}_{1}\,\overline{k_{2}-2}],

and for
k_{1}=1
,

(7.20)

[k_{2}-1]\cdot[\bar{1}\,\overline{k_{2}-1}]=[\bar{1}\,k_{2}-1]\cdot[\overline{k_{2}-1}]+[k_{2}]\cdot[\bar{1}\,\overline{k_{2}-2}].

The first mutation in
\mathbf{T}_{n}
is in column
n
that contains the only mutable vertex
|n,2|
. The exchange relation at
|n,2|
is (
7.20
) for
k_{2}=3
, which produces
{\tt k}_{n,n-1}(X)
. As a result of the mutation at
|n,2|
, the arrow between the vertices in column
n-1
disappears, so mutations at
|n-1,2|
and
|n-1,3|
commute, as mentioned above.
The corresponding exchange relations are (
7.20
) for
k_{2}=4
at
|n-1,2|
, which produces
{\tt k}_{n,n-2}(X)
,
and (
7.19
) for
k_{1}=2
,
k_{2}=3
at
|n-1,3|
, which produces
{\tt k}_{n-1,n-2}(X)
. As a result of these two mutations the arrows between the vertices in column
n-2
disappears, so mutations at the vertices in this column commute.
Following this pattern, we establish that the exchange relation at the vertex
|i,j|
,
j\in[2,n-i+2]
, of column
i

is (
7.20
) for
k_{2}=n-i+4
if
j=2
, which produces
{\tt k}_{n,i-1}
, and (
7.19
) with
k_{1}=j-1
,
k_{2}=n-i-j+4
if
j>2
, which produces
{\tt k}_{n-j+2,i-1}(X)
. Note that for
i=2
,
j=3
, that is, for
k_{1}=2
,
k_{2}=n
,
the function
[\bar{1}\,n]
in the right hand side of (
7.19
) is the product
x_{n1}\det X
.

This concludes the proof of Theorem
2.24
(i). ∎

7.3.
Proof of Theorem
2.24
(ii)

Recall that mutable and frozen variables in the cluster built in the previous section are

(7.21)

\{\phi_{kl}(X):k,l\geq 1,\ k+l\leq n-1\}\cup\{{\tt k}_{ij}(X):1\leq j\leq i\leq n,(i,j)\neq(1,1),(n,1)\}.

and

(7.22)

\{x_{1n},x_{n,1},{\tt k}_{11}(X)=\det X\}\cup\{{\tt c}_{r}/\det X:1\leq r\leq n-1\},

respectively.
Slightly abusing the language, we will say that a funcion of
X
is
Laurent
if it admits a Laurent polynomial expression in terms of the variables in (
7.21
), (
7.22
). Further, a Laurent function is
good Laurent
if this Laurent polynomial expression does not contain frozen variables in the denominator. A matrix is said
to be Laurent (or good Laurent) if every its entry possesses this property. In this terms, our goal is to prove that

X
is good Laurent.

Consider the Gauss factorization
X=X_{+}X_{0,-}
of
X
. We first treat the second factor.

Lemma 7.9
.

X_{0,-}
is good Laurent depending only on
{\tt k}_{ij}(X)
and
x_{n1}
.

Proof.

Clearly

(X_{0,-})_{ii}=(X_{0})_{ii}=\frac{\det X_{[i,n]}^{[i,n]}}{\det X_{[i+1,n]}^{[i+1,n]}}=\frac{{\tt k}_{ii}(X)}{{\tt k}_{i+1,i+1}(X)},\quad 1\leq i\leq n.

For
X,Y\in\operatorname{Mat}_{n}
and
i>j
define

(7.23)

\hat{\tt k}_{ij}(X,Y)=\det\begin{bmatrix}[l]Y_{[i,n]}\ 0&\\
\ 0\ X_{[n+j-i,n]}\hskip-10.00002pt\end{bmatrix}^{[j,n+1]},

so that
{\tt k}_{ij}(X)=\hat{\tt k}_{ij}(X,X)
. Then for any
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{+}
and
\nu\in\mathcal{N}_{-}
,

\hat{\tt k}_{ij}(X,Y)=\hat{\tt k}_{ij}(\beta_{1}X\nu,\beta_{2}Y\gamma(\nu)),

and, in particular,

\displaystyle{\tt k}_{ij}(X)=\hat{\tt k}_{ij}(X,X)

\displaystyle=\hat{\tt k}_{ij}(X_{0,-},X_{0,-})=\hat{\tt k}_{ij}\left(X_{0},X_{0}X_{-}\gamma(X_{-}^{-1})\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(X_{-}\gamma(X_{-}^{-1})\right)_{[i,n]}^{[j,n+j-i]}\det X_{[i,n]}^{[i,n]}\det X_{[n+j-i,n]}^{[n+j-i,n]}.

Denote
N=X_{-}\gamma(X_{-}^{-1})
. We see that

(7.24)

\det N_{[i,n]}^{[j,n+j-i]}=\frac{{\tt k}_{ij}(X)}{{\tt k}_{ii}(X){\tt k}_{n+j-i,n+j-i}(X)},\quad 1\leq j<i\leq n,\ (i,j)\neq(n,1).

Viewed as functions of
N
, minors above together with
N_{n1}=x_{n1}
form an initial cluster for the standard cluster structure on
n\times n
lower unipotent matrices.
It is well-known (for details see
[
15
, Remark 3.4]
, which, in turn, relies on
[
2
]
) that this cluster structure is regular and complete and thus all matrix entries
N_{ij}
have Laurent polynomial expressions in terms of the initial cluster. Being a frozen variable,
N_{n1}=x_{n1}
appears only in the numerators of these expressions. Since

X_{0,-}=X_{0}X_{-}=X_{0}N\gamma(N)\gamma^{2}(N)\dots

and

(7.25)

(X_{0})_{ii}=\frac{{\tt k}_{ii}(X)}{{\tt k}_{i+1,i+1}(X)},\quad i\in[1,n-1],\qquad(X_{0})_{nn}={\tt k}_{nn}(X),

the proof is complete.
∎

We start the treatment of the first factor in the Gauss decomposition of
X
from the following claim.

Lemma 7.10
.

Matrix entries of the first row of
X_{+}
are good Laurent.

Proof.

Since
(X_{+})_{11}=1
and
(X_{+})_{1n}={x_{1n}}/{x_{nn}}={x_{1n}}/{{\tt k}_{nn}(X)}
, we only need to consider

(X_{+})_{1j}
for
j\in[2,n-1]
. Recall the determinantal description for
\phi_{k,n-1-k}
given
at the beginning of Section
4.1
:

\displaystyle\phi_{k,n-1-k}(X)=\det\begin{bmatrix}[l]X_{[n-k,n]}\ 0&\\
\ 0\ X_{1\cup[k+2,n]}\hskip-10.00002pt\end{bmatrix}=\det\begin{bmatrix}[l](X_{0}N)_{[n-k,n]}\ 0&\\
\ 0\ (X_{+}X_{0})_{1\cup[k+2,n]}\hskip-10.00002pt\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\ ={\det X_{[n-k,n]}^{[n-k,n]}}{\det X_{[k+2,n]}^{[k+2,n]}}\det\begin{bmatrix}[l]N_{[n-k,n]}^{[1,k+2]}\\
0\ (X_{+}X_{0})_{1}^{[1,k+1]}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\ ={{\tt k}_{n-k,n-k}}(X){{\tt k}_{k+2,k+2}}(X)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\left((X_{+}X_{0})_{1,k+1}\det N_{[n-k,n]}^{[1,k+1]}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}(-1)^{j+k}(X_{+}X_{0})_{1j}\det N_{[n-k,n]}^{[1,k+1]\setminus\{j+1\}}\right)

\displaystyle\ ={\tt k}_{n-k,1}(X)\cdot(X_{+})_{1,k+1}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+{{\tt k}_{n-k,n-k}}(X){{\tt k}_{k+2,k+2}}(X)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k}(-1)^{j+k}(X_{+}X_{0})_{1j}\det N_{[n-k,n]}^{[1,k+1]\setminus\{j+1\}}\right);

in the last equality we used (
7.24
) for
i=n-k
and
j=1
and (
7.25
) for
i=k+1
.
The resulting formulas for
\phi_{1,n-2}(X)
,
\phi_{2,n-3}(X),\dots,\phi_{n-1,1}(X)
can be viewed as a triangular system of linear equations for
(X_{+})_{12},\ldots,(X_{+})_{1,n-1}
. Due to Lemma
7.9
, the coefficients of this system are good Laurent, and in solving the system recursively for
(X_{+})_{12},\ldots,(X_{+})_{1,n-1}
, one only needs to divide by
{\tt k}_{n-k,1}(X),\ldots,{\tt k}_{21}(X)
, which proves the claim.
∎

To obtain required Laurent polynomial expressions for the remaining matrix entries of
X_{+}
, we first generalize the map

\Psi^{\prime}:GL_{n}\to GL_{n-1},\quad\Psi^{\prime}:X\mapsto U(X)=X_{L}X^{-1}_{R}

from Section
3.1
to a map

{\widehat{\Psi}}^{\prime}:GL_{n}\times GL_{n}\to GL_{n-1},\quad{\widehat{\Psi}}^{\prime}:(X,Y)\mapsto{\widehat{U}}(X,Y)=X_{L}Y^{-1}_{R}

and observe that it satisfies the following easily verifiable invariance property:

{\widehat{U}}(\gamma(\beta)X\nu,\beta Y\gamma(\nu))=\beta_{[1,n-1]}^{[1,n-1]}{\widehat{U}}(X,Y)\left(\beta_{[1,n-1]}^{[1,n-1]}\right)^{-1}

for any
\beta\in\mathcal{N}_{+}
and
\nu\in\mathcal{N}_{-}
. Therefore,

(7.26)

\displaystyle U(X)

\displaystyle={\widehat{U}}\left(X_{+}X_{0,-},X_{+}X_{0,-}\right)

\displaystyle=(X^{-1}_{+})_{[1,n-1]}^{[1,n-1]}{\widehat{U}}\left(\gamma(X^{-1}_{+})X_{+}X_{0},X_{0}X_{-}\gamma(X_{-}^{-1})\right)(X_{+})_{[1,n-1]}^{[1,n-1]}.

Denote

(7.27)

V=\gamma(X^{-1}_{+})X_{+}.

Since the functions (
2.8
) are invariant under conjugation by upper unipotent elements of
GL_{n-1}
, the formula above implies

(7.28)

\varphi_{kl}(U(X))=\varphi_{kl}\left({\widehat{U}}\left(VX_{0},X_{0}N)\right)\right).

To better understand the structure of
\widehat{\mathrm{U}}={\widehat{U}}\left(VX_{0},X_{0}N\right)
, observe that for an upper triangular matrix
B
and a lower triangular matrix
C
,

\displaystyle B_{L}

\displaystyle=B_{[2,n]}^{[2,n]}\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathbf{1}_{n-2}\\
-\frac{b_{11}}{b_{1n}}&\cdots-\frac{b_{1,n-1}}{b_{1n}}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle C^{-1}_{R}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}-\frac{c_{n2}}{c_{n1}}&\cdots-\frac{c_{nn}}{c_{n1}}\\
\mathbf{1}_{n-2}&0\\
\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\tilde{C}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathbf{1}_{n-2}\\
-\frac{c_{n1}}{c_{nn}}&\cdots-\frac{c_{n,n-1}}{c_{nn}}\end{bmatrix}\tilde{C}^{-1}

with
\tilde{C}=C_{[1,n-1]}^{[1,n-1]}
.
Consequently, if we choose
B=VX_{0}
,
C=X_{0}N
, then

\widehat{\mathrm{U}}=B_{[2,n]}^{[2,n]}\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathbf{1}_{n-2}\\
-\frac{(X_{+,0})_{11}}{x_{1n}}&\cdots-\frac{(X_{+,0})_{1,n-1}}{x_{1n}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathbf{1}_{n-2}\\
-N_{n1}&\cdots-N_{n,n-1}\end{bmatrix}\tilde{C}^{-1}.

Denote
M=\left(B_{[2,n]}^{[2,n]}\right)^{-1}\hat{\mathrm{U}}
,
and let

(7.29)

D_{1}=\mbox{diag}(\mathbf{1}_{n-2},x_{1n}),\quad D_{2}=\mbox{diag}(\mathbf{1}_{n-2},\det X).

Lemma 7.11
.

The matrices

\left(Mw_{0}\right)^{-1}_{+}
and
\left(Mw_{0}\right)^{-1}_{0,-}(D_{1}D_{2})^{-1}

are good Laurent.

Proof.

By Lemmas
7.9
and
7.10
, all matrix entries of
M
are Laurent. Furthermore, after multiplying
M
by
D_{1}
on the left and by
w_{0}D_{2}w_{0}^{-1}=\mbox{diag}(\det X,\mathbf{1}_{n-2})
on the right, all the matrix entries of the resulting matrix
\widetilde{M}
become good Laurent.

Further,
\left(Mw_{0}\right)_{0,-}=(\widehat{\mathrm{U}}w_{0})_{0,-}
, so by (
7.26
), (
2.8
),
and (
2.17
),

\displaystyle\det(Mw_{0})_{[i,n-1]}^{[i,n-1]}

\displaystyle=\det\widehat{\mathrm{U}}_{[i,n-1]}^{[1,n-i]}=\det U(X)_{[i,n-1]}^{[1,n-i]}=\pm\frac{\phi_{i-1,n-i}(X)}{x_{1n}\det X},

\displaystyle\det(\widetilde{M}w_{0})_{[i,n-1]}^{[i,n-1]}

\displaystyle=\det(D_{1}Mw_{0}D_{2})_{[i,n-1]}^{[i,n-1]}=\pm\phi_{i-1,n-i}(X).

Recall that the denominators that enter the Gauss factorization of a matrix are its principal trailing minors, hence
matrix entries of all factors in the Gauss factorization of
Mw_{0}
are Laurent, and those of
\widetilde{M}w_{0}
are good Laurent. Consequently,
\left(Mw_{0}\right)_{+}=D_{1}^{-1}(\widetilde{M}w_{0})_{+}D_{1}
is also good Laurent and so is its inverse. Finally,
\left(Mw_{0}\right)_{0,-}=D_{1}^{-1}(\widetilde{M}w_{0})_{0,-}D_{2}^{-1}
and so

\left(Mw_{0}\right)^{-1}_{0,-}(D_{1}D_{2})^{-1}=D_{2}(\widetilde{M}w_{0})^{-1}_{0,-}D_{2}^{-1}

is good Laurent.
∎

In order to restore the matrix entries of
B_{[2,n]}^{[2,n]}
as Laurent polynomials in (
7.21
), (
7.22
), we need to briefly review some technical results from
[
15
]
. (Note that the statements and formulas we will need are obtained from their counterparts in
[
15
]
via consistently applied conjugation
by
w_{0}
and replacing
n
with
n-1
.) Let
w_{c}=E_{12}+\ldots+E_{n-2,n-1}+E_{n-1,1}

be a representative in
SL_{n-1}
of the Coxeter element
s_{n-2}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{n-1}
. By
[
15
, Lemma 8.2]
, one can uniquely represent
\widehat{\mathrm{U}}
as

\widehat{\mathrm{U}}=\hat{\beta}(\hat{\nu}w_{c}){\hat{\beta}}^{-1}

with
\hat{\beta}\in\mathcal{N}_{+}
,
\hat{\nu}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{-}
depending rationally on matrix entries of
\widehat{\mathrm{U}}
.
Moreover, by
[
15
, Section 7.3.2]
, the matrix entries of
\hat{\nu}=\left(\hat{\nu}_{ij}\right)_{i,j=1}^{n-1}
are Laurent polynomials in terms of functions
\varphi_{kl}(\widehat{\mathrm{U}})
.
The latter claim follows from the formulas
[
15
, equation (5.8)]
that can be rewritten as

(7.30)

\displaystyle\varphi_{kl}(\widehat{\mathrm{U}})=\pm\det\hat{\nu}^{[1,l-1]}_{[k+1,k+l-1]}\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{n-k-l},\quad l>1,k+l\leq n-1,

\displaystyle\varphi_{k1}(\widehat{\mathrm{U}})=\pm\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{n-k-1},\quad k\in[1,n-2],

where

\eta_{s}=\hat{\nu}_{n-1,n-1}\cdots\hat{\nu}_{n-s,n-s}
for
s\in[1,n-2]
.
From (
7.30
) one obtains monomial expressions in terms of functions
\varphi_{kl}(\widehat{\mathrm{U}})
for the diagonal entries and flag minors of
\hat{\nu}_{[1,n-2]}^{[1,n-2]}
:

(7.31)

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}_{11}=\pm\frac{x_{n1}\varphi_{21}(\widehat{\mathrm{U}})}{x_{1n}\varphi_{11}(\widehat{\mathrm{U}})},

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}_{kk}=\pm\frac{\varphi_{k-1,1}(\widehat{\mathrm{U}})\varphi_{k+1,1}(\widehat{\mathrm{U}})}{\varphi_{k1}(\widehat{\mathrm{U}})^{2}},\quad k\in[2,n-2],

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}_{n-1,n-1}=\pm\varphi_{n-2,1}(\widehat{\mathrm{U}}),

\displaystyle\det\hat{\nu}^{[1,l-1]}_{[k+1,k+l-1]}=\pm\frac{\varphi_{kl}(\widehat{\mathrm{U}})}{\varphi_{k+l-1,1}(\widehat{\mathrm{U}})},\quad l\leq n-1,k<n-l.

These form an initial cluster for the standard cluster structure on
(n-2)\times(n-2)
lower triangular matrices which is well known to be complete (see the proof of Lemma
7.9
). This ensures that the matrix entries of

\hat{\nu}_{[1,n-2]}^{[1,n-2]}
are Laurent polynomials in (
7.21
), (
7.22
).
Finally, Laurent polynomial expressions for
the last row of
\hat{\nu}
were obtained in
[
15
, Section 7.3.2]
by representing the entries

\hat{\nu}_{n-1,j}
,
j\in[1,n-2]
as polynomials in off-diagonal entries of
\hat{\nu}_{[1,n-2]}^{[1,n-2]}
and coefficients
c_{1}(\widehat{\mathrm{U}}),\ldots,c_{n-2}(\widehat{\mathrm{U}})
divided by monomials in
\hat{\nu}_{kk}
,
k\in[1,n-2]
.

Lemma 7.12
.

The matrix
B_{[2,n]}^{[2,n]}
in (
7.26
) can be written as

B_{[2,n]}^{[2,n]}=w_{0}(Mw_{0})^{-1}_{0,-}(\hat{\nu}w_{c}w_{0})_{0,-}w_{0}^{-1}(\hat{\nu}w_{c}w_{0})_{+}(Mw_{0})_{+}^{-1}.

Proof.

Recall that
\widehat{\mathrm{U}}=B^{[2,n]}_{[2,n]}M=\hat{\beta}(\hat{\nu}w_{c}){\hat{\beta}}^{-1}
. Multiplying by
w_{0}
on the right and taking the Gauss factorization of both sides we arrive at

B^{[2,n]}_{[2,n]}(Mw_{0})_{+}(Mw_{0})_{0,-}=\hat{\beta}(\hat{\nu}w_{c}w_{0})_{+}(\hat{\nu}w_{c}w_{0})_{0,-}w_{0}^{-1}{\hat{\beta}}^{-1}w_{0}.

Comparing the factors in the latter equality, we obtain

\hat{\beta}^{-1}=w_{0}(\hat{\nu}w_{c}w_{0})_{0,-}^{-1}(Mw_{0})_{0,-}w_{0}^{-1}

and

B^{[2,n]}_{[2,n]}=\hat{\beta}(\hat{\nu}w_{c}w_{0})_{+}(Mw_{0})_{+}^{-1}=w_{0}(Mw_{0})^{-1}_{0,-}(\hat{\nu}w_{c}w_{0})_{0,-}w_{0}^{-1}(\hat{\nu}w_{c}w_{0})_{+}(Mw_{0})_{+}^{-1}.

∎

We are now ready to complete the proof that
X
is good Laurent.
Indeed the first row of
V
defined in (
7.27
) coincides with the first row of
X_{+}
, while

V_{[2,n]}^{[2,n]}=B^{[2,n]}_{[2,n]}\left((X_{0})^{[2,n]}_{[2,n]}\right)^{-1}
.
If
V
is good Laurent, then the same is true about
X_{+}=\cdots\gamma^{2}(V)\gamma(V)V
and, due to Lemma
7.9
, about
X=X_{+}X_{0,-}
.

In view of Lemmas
7.9
and
7.10
, to verify that
X
is good Laurent, we only need to establish a similar claim for
B^{[2,n]}_{[2,n]}
. First, we analyze how the frozen variables
x_{n1}
,
x_{1n}
,
and
\det X
enter the Laurent polynomial expressions for the matrix entries
\hat{\nu}_{ij}
discussed above. Observe that by (
7.28
) and (
2.17
), equations (
7.31
) can be re-written as

(7.32)

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}_{11}=\pm\frac{x_{n1}\det X\phi_{21}(X)}{\phi_{11}(X)},

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}_{kk}=\pm\frac{\phi_{k-1,1}(X)\phi_{k+1,1}(X)}{\phi_{k1}(X)^{2}},\quad k\in[2,n-2],

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}_{n-1,n-1}=\pm\frac{\phi_{n-2,1}(X)}{x_{1n}\det X},

\displaystyle\det\hat{\nu}^{[1,l-1]}_{[k+1,k+l-1]}=\pm\frac{\phi_{kl}(X)}{\phi_{k+l-1,1}(X)},\quad l\leq n-1,k<n-l.

This means, in particular, that expressions for entries of the rows 2 through
n-2
of
\hat{\nu}
do not contain frozen variables at all.

Let us look more closely at the Laurent polynomial expressions for the entries of the last row of
\hat{\nu}
, following the strategy of
[
15
, Section 7.3.2]
.
It involves a conjugation of
\hat{\nu}w_{c}
to its companion form performed in two steps. The first is the diagonal conjugation by

T=\mbox{diag}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n-1}):=\mbox{diag}(\hat{\nu}_{11}\cdots\hat{\nu}_{n-2,n-2},\hat{\nu}_{22}\cdots\hat{\nu}_{n-2,n-2},\ldots,\hat{\nu}_{n-2,n-2},1)

that results in
T^{-1}\hat{\nu}w_{c}T=\begin{bmatrix}0&\zeta\\
\varkappa&\xi\end{bmatrix}
, where

\varkappa=\hat{\nu}_{11}\cdots\hat{\nu}_{n-1,n-1}=\det\hat{\nu}=\pm\det U(X)=\pm\frac{x_{n1}}{x_{1n}},\quad\xi=(\hat{\nu}_{n-1,j}t_{j+1})_{j=1}^{n-2},

and
\zeta
is a unipotent
(n-2)\times(n-2)
lower triangular matrix whose off-diagonal entries
\zeta_{ij}=\hat{\nu}_{ij}t_{i}^{-1}t_{j+1}
,
1\leq j<i<n-1
, are good Laurent by the second and the fourth equations
in (
7.32
). The second step is the conjugation by the matrix

\eta=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&\zeta^{-1}\gamma(\zeta^{-1})\gamma^{2}(\zeta^{-1})\cdots\end{bmatrix}
. The resulting matrix

\eta^{-1}T^{-1}\hat{\nu}w_{c}T\eta
is in a companion form with coefficients of the characteristic polynomial in the last row. Since
\hat{\nu}w_{c}
is similar to
U(X)
, the last row of this companion matrix is made of functions

\pm c_{p}(U(X))
. We conclude that the matrix entries
\hat{\nu}_{n-1,j}
,
j\in[1,n-2]
are represented by expressions that are linear in
c_{i}(U(X))
, polynomial in
\zeta_{ij}
, and Laurent polynomial in
\hat{\nu}_{ii}
,
i\in[2,n-2]
.
By (
2.19
),

c_{p}(U(X))=\frac{{\tt c}_{p}(X)}{x_{1n}\det X}
. Together with the third equation in (
7.32
), this means that every matrix entry of the last row of
\hat{\nu}
is a good Laurent function divided by
x_{1n}\det X
. To summarize, the matrix
\bar{\nu}=D_{1}D_{2}\hat{\nu}
, where
D_{1},D_{2}
are defined in (
7.29
), is good Laurent.

Now, consider the Gauss factorization of
\bar{\nu}w_{c}w_{0}
. The trailing principal minors are

\det(\bar{\nu}w_{c}w_{0})_{[i,n-1]}^{[i,n-1]}=\pm\bar{\nu}_{n-1,n-1}\det(\bar{\nu})_{[i,n-2]}^{[1,n-i-1]}\\
=\pm x_{1n}\det X\hat{\nu}_{n-1,n-1}\det(\hat{\nu})_{[i,n-2]}^{[1,n-i-1]}=\pm\phi_{n-2,1}(X)\frac{\phi_{i-1,n-i}(X)}{\phi_{n-2,1}(X)}.

Thus, the factors in the Gauss factorization of
\bar{\nu}w_{c}w_{0}
are good Laurent, and so is

(\hat{\nu}w_{c}w_{0})_{+}=(D_{1}D_{2})^{-1}(\bar{\nu}w_{c}w_{0})_{+}D_{1}D_{2}
, while

(\hat{\nu}w_{c}w_{0})_{0,-}=(D_{1}D_{2})^{-1}(\bar{\nu}w_{c}w_{0})_{0,-}
is Laurent, but not good Laurent. However, we can rewrite the equation for
B_{[2,n]}^{[2,n]}
from Lemma
7.12
as follows:

\displaystyle B_{[2,n]}^{[2,n]}

\displaystyle=w_{0}(Mw_{0})^{-1}_{0,-}(\hat{\nu}w_{c}w_{0})_{0,-}w_{0}^{-1}(\hat{\nu}w_{c}w_{0})_{+}(Mw_{0})_{+}^{-1}

\displaystyle=w_{0}\left((Mw_{0})^{-1}_{0,-}(D_{1}D_{2})^{-1}\right)(\bar{\nu}w_{c}w_{0})_{0,-}w_{0}^{-1}(\hat{\nu}w_{c}w_{0})_{+}(Mw_{0})_{+}^{-1}.

Taking into account Lemma
7.11
, we see that in the last expression above all factors are good Laurent, which concludes the proof. ∎

7.4.
Final steps in the proof of Theorem
2.17

To complete the proof of Theorem
2.17
we have to check that conditions of Proposition
2.22
are valid. We will apply this proposition to the Laurent representations obtained in Theorems
2.23
and
2.24
(ii). Therefore, we have to check
coprimality for any polynomial in the family
\left\{{\tt g}_{ii}(X),i\in[2,n-1];x_{1n};x_{n1};\det X\right\}
, with any
polynomial in the family

\left\{{\tt k}_{ij}(X),1\leq j\leq i\leq n,(i,j)\neq(n,1),(1,1);\phi_{kl}(X),k,l\in[1,n-2],k+l\leq n-1\right\}
.

We start from the following statement.

Proposition 7.13
.

Polynomials
{\tt g}_{ii}(X)
,
i\in[2,n-1]
, are irreducible.

Proof.

Note that
{\tt g}_{n-1,n-1}(X)
equals, up to a sign, to the resultant of polynomials

\sum_{t=0}^{n-1}x_{1,n-t}z^{t}
and
\sum_{t=0}^{n-1}x_{n,n-t}z^{t}
, which is classically known to be irreducible
(see, e. g.,
[
24
, Ch. 5.9]
).

For
i\in[2,n-2]
, assume that
{\tt g}_{ii}(Y)
are irreducible for
Y
of size
k\times k
with
k\in[i+1,n-1]
(note that for
k=i+1
,
{\tt g}_{ii}(Y)
is once again a resultant), and consider
{\tt g}_{ii}(X)
. Clearly,

{\tt g}_{ii}(X)
is linear in all variables
x_{i+1,l}
for
l\in[1,n]
. Let
c_{i+1,n}
denote the coefficient at
x_{i+1,n}
in
{\tt g}_{ii}(X)
. It is easy to see that
c_{i+1,n}
restricted to
x_{1n}=x_{nn}=0
equals

{\tt g}_{ii}(Y)
for the matrix
Y
obtained from
X
by deleting the
(i+1)
th row and the last column, and hence is irreducible by the assumption, so that
c_{i+1,n}
itself is irreducible as well. Consequently, if
{\tt g}_{ii}(X)=PQ
and
P
depends on
x_{i+1,n}
, then either
P=x_{i+1,n}c_{i+1,n}+\dots
, and hence
Q=1
, or
P=x_{i+1,n}+\dots
, and hence
Q=c_{i+1,n}
. The latter case is impossible, since
x_{i+1,n-1}
enters
P
together with
x_{i+1,n}
, so that
PQ
would contain a monomial with
x_{i+1,n-1}x_{1,n-1}^{i}
, while
{\tt g}_{ii}(X)
only contains monomials with

x_{i+1,n-1}x_{1,n-1}^{k}
for
k<i
.
∎

Note that all polynomials in the first family, except for
x_{n1}
, depend on the entries of the first row of
X
, while all polynomials
{\tt k}_{ij}(X)
in the second family do not depend on the entries of this row and are not divisible by
x_{n1}
. Consequently, by Proposition
7.13
, polynomials
{\tt k}_{ij}(X)
in the second family are coprime with all polynomials in the first family. Further, polynomials
\phi_{kl}(X)
are not divisible by any of

{\tt g}_{ii}(X)
. Indeed, all
{\tt g}_{ii}(X)
vanish identically on
x_{11}=x_{n1}=0
. On the other hand, none of

\phi_{kl}(X)
have this property: it would imply that there exists a non-trivial linear combination of rows of

\left.\Phi_{kl}(X)\right|_{x_{11}=x_{n1}=0}
representing zero, which is impossible, since the entries of each column of this matrix are distinct independent variables. In a similar way, the fact that
\phi_{kl}(X)
are not divisible
by
\det X
follows from the fact that the latter vanishes identically for
x_{12}=x_{22}=\dots=x_{n2}=0
, while the former do not. Clearly,
\phi_{kl}(X)
are not divisible by
x_{1n}
and
x_{n1}
either, so the required coprimality
follows once again from Proposition
7.13
.

Remark 7.14
.

In fact, one can use the above reasoning together with
[
25
, Lemma 5.5]
to prove that all functions in both families are irreducible.

We can now check that the ground ring for the obtained generalized cluster algebra is indeed
{\mathbb{C}}[x_{1n},x_{n1},\det X^{\pm 1}]
, as indicated in Remark
2.3
. To achieve this goal we will prove that the Laurent representations for the matrix entries obtained in Section
7.1
, in fact, do not contain
x_{1n}
,
x_{n1}
, and
\det X
in the denominator. This statement is based on the following observation.

Proposition 7.15
.

If the Laurent representation of an element of a generalized upper cluster algebra in some seed
\Sigma
does not contain frozen variables in the denominator then the same holds for Laurent representations in all seeds.

Proof.

Clearly, it is enough to prove that the Laurent representation in the adjacent seed
\overline{\Sigma}
obtained by replacing a cluster variable
f
by
\bar{f}
has this property. Let
R
and
\bar{R}
be the rings of polynomials in the cluster variables of
\Sigma
and
\overline{\Sigma}
, respectively. Assume that
x=QM/f^{k}
, where
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
Q\in R
is not divisible by a monomial and
M
is a Laurent monomial in cluster variables of
\Sigma
excluding
f
and not containing frozen variables in the denominator. Further, let
x=\bar{Q}\bar{M}
, where
\bar{Q}\in\bar{R}
is not divisible by a monomial and
\bar{M}
is a Laurent monomial in cluster variables of

\overline{\Sigma}
. Recall that
f
and
\bar{f}
satisfy the exchange relation
f\bar{f}=P
, where
P\in R\cap\bar{R}
.
Let
m
be the minimal non-negative integer such that
\tilde{Q}=\bar{f}^{m}\left.Q\right|_{f=P/\bar{f}}\in\bar{R}
, then

\tilde{Q}\bar{f}^{k-m}M=\bar{Q}\bar{M}P^{k}
is an identity in
\bar{R}
. Since cluster variables in
\overline{\Sigma}
are algebraically independent, this identity is only possible if
\tilde{Q}=\bar{Q}P^{k}
and
\bar{M}=\bar{f}^{k-m}M
, therefore,
\bar{M}
retains the property of not containing frozen variables in the denominator.
∎

Taking into account that Laurent representations for the matrix elements in Theorem
2.24
do not contain frozen variables in the denominator, we get the required statement. ∎

Remark 7.16
.

We can now check that unfreezing vertex
D
was imminent, as indicated in Section
2.7
.4. Indeed, by
Proposition
2.16
, the bracket between the cluster variable
{\tt g}_{n-1,n-1}(X)
at
D
and the function
{\tt g}(X)
that replaces it after the mutation at
D
is given by

\{{\tt g}_{n-1,n-1}(X),{\tt g}(X)\}=c_{1}{\tt f}_{2n-3}(X){\tt g}_{n-2,n-2}(X)+c_{2}x_{n1}{\tt f}_{2n-4}(X){\tt g}_{n-1,n-2}(X)

for some constants
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
. Computations in the proof of Proposition
6.3
show that
c_{2}-c_{1}=1
, so that

\frac{\{{\tt g}_{n-1,n-1}(X),{\tt g}(X)\}}{{\tt g}_{n-1,n-1}(X)}=c_{2}{\tt g}(X)-\frac{{\tt f}_{2n-3}(X){\tt g}_{n-2,n-2}(X)}{{\tt g}_{n-1,n-1}(X)}.

By Proposition
7.13
, polynomials
{\tt g}_{n-1,n-1}(X)
and
{\tt g}_{n-2,n-2}(X)
are irreducible. Further,
{\operatorname{deg}}\,{\tt f}_{2n-3}(X)<{\operatorname{deg}}\,{\tt g}_{n-1,n-1}(X)
, hence the expression above is not a regular function on

GL_{n}
, and Proposition
2.15
applies.

7.5.
Proof of Theorem
2.25

We perform the regular pullback of the generalized structure
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
on
GL_{n}
to
\operatorname{Mat}_{n}
as
described in
[
20
]
, taking
g_{1}=\det X
as the distinguished polynomial.
The function
\varphi_{1}(r)
computed via the first relation in
[
20
, Eq. (4.5)]
equals 1 for
r\in[1,n-2]

and vanishes for
r=0,n-1
, and

\tau_{1}
computed via the second relation in
[
20
, Eq. (4.5)]
vanishes. Therefore, we are exactly within
conditions of
[
20
, Remark 4.6]
, which stipulate one arrow between
B
and
(1,1)
in both directions in the
quiver
\widehat{Q}_{n}
and the rules of its mutations as described in Section
2.7
.6.

The compatibility of
\widehat{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n}
with
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
is an immediate consequence of the compatibility of
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}
with
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
, since
\det X
is a Casimir for
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
. Regularity of
\widehat{{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}}_{n}
follows from the regularity of
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
. To prove the completeness we invoke
[
20
, Theorem 6.2]
. By Theorem
2.24
(ii), every matrix element

x_{ij}
of
X
can be written as a Laurent polynomial in the cluster variables of the seed described in part (i) of the same theorem, and these Laurent polynomials do not contain frozen variables, in particular
\det X
, in the denominator.
Therefore, every
x_{ij}
can be written as a Laurent polynomial in these cluster variables at a generic point of the
hypersurface
\{\det X=0\}
, and
[
20
, Theorem 6.2]
applies. ∎

Appendix: Regular complete cluster structure on the space
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
of rational functions

The goal of this appendix is to prove Theorem
2.8
.
We will utilize a map
\rho
from
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
to
{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
that sends

\bar{M}(\lambda)=\frac{\bar{q}(\lambda)}{p(\lambda)}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{\bar{h}_{i}}{\lambda^{i}}

with
\bar{q}(\lambda)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\bar{q}_{i}\lambda^{i}
,
p(\lambda)=\lambda^{n}+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}p_{i}\lambda^{i}

to

M(\lambda)=\bar{M}(\lambda)-\bar{q}_{n}=\bar{M}(\lambda)-\bar{h}_{0}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{h_{i}}{\lambda^{i+1}}=\frac{q(\lambda)}{p(\lambda)},

so that
h_{n}=\bar{h}_{n+1}
for
n=0,1,\ldots
and
q(\lambda)=\bar{q}(\lambda)-\bar{q}_{n}\lambda^{n}
.

The map
\rho
can be realized by a sequence of cluster mutations in the following way. First, note that the cluster variable
\bar{t}_{m}^{+}

attached to the vertex
(m,+)
in
\bar{Q}_{n}^{T}
coincides with
t_{m}^{-}
for
m=1,\ldots,n
.
Apply to the initial seed
(\bar{\mathcal{T}}_{n},\bar{Q}_{n}^{T})
the sequence
\mu
of mutations at vertices

(1,-),(2,-),\ldots,(n,-)
. The cluster variable
\bar{t}_{m}^{-}
attached to the vertex
(m,-)
then transforms into

(\bar{t}_{m}^{-})^{\prime}=\det(\bar{h}_{\alpha+\beta})_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{m}=\det(\bar{H}_{m+1}^{+})_{\hat{1}}^{\hat{1}}=\det H_{m}^{+}=t_{m}^{+}

for
m=1,\ldots,n-1
and into

\frac{t_{m}^{+}}{t_{m}^{-}}=\frac{\det(\bar{h}_{\alpha+\beta})_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{m}}{\det(\bar{h}_{\alpha+\beta-1})_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{m}}

for
m=n
.

Indeed, it is straightforward to see that the cluster transformations involved in
\mu
are given by

\displaystyle\bar{t}_{1}^{-}(\bar{t}_{1}^{-})^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\bar{t}_{2}^{-}+(\bar{t}_{1}^{+})^{2},

\displaystyle\bar{t}_{m}^{-}(\bar{t}_{m}^{-})^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\bar{t}_{m+1}^{-}(\bar{t}_{m-1}^{-})^{\prime}+(\bar{t}_{m}^{+})^{2},\quad m\in[2,n-1],

\displaystyle\bar{t}_{n}^{-}(\bar{t}_{n}^{-})^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{\bar{t}_{m+1}^{-}}{\bar{t}_{n}^{+}}(\bar{t}_{m-1}^{-})^{\prime}+\bar{t}_{n}^{+}\ .

Then the claim follows from the Desnanot–Jacobi identity for Hankel determinants, which in our notations takes a form

\bar{t}_{m}^{-}t_{m}^{+}=\bar{t}_{m+1}^{-}t_{m-1}^{+}+(\bar{t}_{m}^{+})^{2}.

We see that cluster variables attached to vertices
(m,\pm)
,
m\in[1,n]
, in the seed
\mu(\bar{\mathcal{T}}_{n},\bar{Q}_{n}^{T})
coincide with cluster variables attached to the same vertices in
({\mathcal{T}}_{n},Q_{n}^{T})
. In fact, it is easy to check that the restriction of
\mu(\bar{Q}_{n}^{T})
to the full subquiver on vertices
(m,\pm)
,
m\in[1,n]
, coincides with
Q_{n}^{T}
, if we consider the vertex
(n,+)
frozen. Now we can invoke results of
[
11
]
about the cluster structure
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{T}
on

{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
defined by the seed
({\mathcal{T}}_{n},Q_{n}^{T})
to establish the required properties of
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{T}
.

We start with regularity. Recall that the coefficients
\bar{h}_{i}
in the Laurent expansion of
\bar{M}(\lambda)
are polynomials in coefficients of
p(\lambda)
and
\bar{q}(\lambda)
, as can be seen from (
4.15
),
and that
\bar{t}_{n}^{+}
does not vanish on
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
due to the classical criterion of coprimality of
p(\lambda)
and

\bar{q}(\lambda)
, see, e.g.,
[
8
, Theorem 8.39]
. Therefore, mutations that constitute our sequence
\mu
yield regular functions on
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
. If
\mu
is followed up with mutations in the remaining vertices
(1,+),\ldots,(n-1,+)
, the resulting cluster variables are also regular on
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
as pullbacks by the map
\rho
of cluster variables in the structure
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{T}
known to be regular. As explained in
[
7
, Section 9]
, this guarantees regularity of
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{T}
.

Next, we will show that
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{T}
is complete over the ground ring
{\mathbb{C}}[\bar{t}_{n+1}^{-}/\bar{t}_{n}^{+},(\bar{t}_{n}^{+})^{\pm 1}]
, as indicated in Remark
2.9
. By
[
11
, Lemma 5.3]
,
h_{i}
,
i\in[0,2n-1]
, are cluster variables in

{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{T}
, and so
\bar{h}_{i}
,
i\in[0,2n]
, are cluster variables in
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{T}
. We can rewrite equations (
4.15
) for
j\in[1,n]
as

(A.1)

[\bar{q}_{n},\ldots,\bar{q}_{1}]=[\bar{h}_{0},\ldots,\bar{h}_{n-1}]\begin{bmatrix}1&p_{n-1}&p_{n-2}&\cdots&p_{1}\\
0&1&p_{n-1}&p_{n-2}&\cdots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&1&p_{n-1}\\
0&\cdots&0&0&1\end{bmatrix},

and those for
j\in[-n,0]
as

(A.2)

[\bar{q}_{0},0,\ldots,0]=[p_{0},\ldots,p_{n-1},1]\bar{H}^{-}_{n+1}.

The latter equation implies that

[p_{0},\ldots,p_{n-1}]=-[\bar{h}_{n+1},\ldots,\bar{h}_{2n}]\left(\bar{H}^{+}_{n}\right)^{-1}

and
\bar{q}_{0}=\bar{t}_{n+1}^{-}/\bar{t}_{n}^{+}
.
Since the entries of
\bar{H}^{-}_{n+1}
are cluster variables in
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{T}
and
\det\bar{H}^{+}_{n}=\bar{t}_{n}^{+}
is a frozen variable, we conclude that coefficients
p_{0},\ldots,p_{n-1}
belong to the corresponding cluster algebra. The same conclusion for
\bar{q}_{1},\ldots,\bar{q}_{n}
follows from (
A.1
).

To complete the proof, we need to show compatibility of
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{T}
with
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{T}
. Once again, we will rely on results from
[
11
]
. Since, by (
2.13
), and (
2.15
), coefficients
h_{i}
and
\bar{h}_{i}
satisfy the same Poisson relations with respect to
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{T}
, the same is true for Poisson brackets between Hankel determinants built from
h_{i}
and
\bar{h}_{i}
respectively. In the case of
{\mathcal{R}}_{n}
, these were computed in
[
11
]
as a part of the proof of compatibility of
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{T}
with
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{T}
.
We will now use the following straightforward observation.
Let
Q
be a quiver in a cluster structure compatible with a Poisson bracket
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
. Suppose we apply a monomial transformation to cluster variables attached to frozen variables in
Q
. Then to adjust
Q
to a new quiver
\tilde{Q}
so that the seed with unchanged mutable variables and the new frozen variables remains compatible with
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}
, one needs to change arrows between frozen and mutable vertices in
Q
in such a way that all
y
-variables at mutable vertices remain unchanged. We apply this observation to
Q^{T}_{n+1}
in which functions attached to the vertices
(m,\pm)
are
\bar{t}_{m}^{\pm}

instead of
t_{m}^{\pm}
.
We then freeze the vertex
(n,+)
, which detaches the frozen vertex
(n+1,+)
from the quiver. The only
y
-variable that involves the frozen vertex
(n+1,-)

is associated with vertex
(n,-)
and is equal to

\frac{(\bar{t}_{n}^{+})^{2}\bar{t}^{-}_{n-1}}{(\bar{t}_{n-1}^{+})^{2}\bar{t}^{-}_{n+1}}=\frac{\bar{t}_{n}^{+}\bar{t}^{-}_{n-1}}{(\bar{t}_{n-1}^{+})^{2}(\bar{t}^{-}_{n+1}/\bar{t}_{n}^{+})}.

Therefore, if one replaces the frozen variable
\bar{t}_{n+1}^{-}
with
\bar{t}_{n+1}^{-}/\bar{t}_{n}^{+}
, the requirement of compatibility with
{\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\}}^{T}
leads one to recover the quiver
\bar{Q}^{T}_{n}
. This completes the proof. ∎
